Deficit and ( q , t ) (q,t) -symmetry in triangular partitions
Loïc Le Mogne, Viviane Pons

Abstract
. We study the ( q , t ) (q,t) -enumeration of triangular Dyck paths considered by Bergeron and Mazin. To do so, we introduce the notion of triangular and sim-sym tableaux and the deficit statistic which is a new interpretation of the dinv . We use it to obtain new results and proofs on triangular 2 2 -partitions and an interesting conjecture for a certain lattice interval ( q , t , r ) (q,t,r) -enumeration.



1.
Introduction

In
[
BM23
]
, Bergeron and Mazin study a certain family of partitions that they call
triangular partitions
, motivated by the results of
[
BHM+23
]
. A triangular partition is the maximal partition lying under a given line. The sub-partitions of a triangular partition are called the triangular Dyck paths, generalizing classical and rational Dyck paths. The
(q,t)
-enumeration of paths, such as the
(q,t)
-Catalan numbers
[
Hag08
]
, has raised interesting combinatorial questions in recent years, related in particular to representation theory. The Negut formula (see for instance
[
Ber22
,
GHSR20
,
GN15
]
) gives a
(q,t)
-enumeration of the sub-partitions of any given partition but the signification of each
(q,t)
monomial is not clear and the coefficients are not always positive. In a recent work generalizing the
shuffle theorem

[
BHM+23
]
, the authors found out a combinatorial interpretation when the partition is triangular, using two statistics generalizing the classical
area
and
dinv
of a Dyck path. Indeed, in this case the
(q,t)
polynomials appear as the coefficients of some symmetric functions and are by nature symmetric themselves. More generally, Conjecture 7.1.1 from
[
BHM+23
]
states that the Negut formula gives positive coefficients if the partition lies under a certain convex curve. As expressed in
[
Ber22
]
, Bergeron further conjectures that this condition is not only sufficient but necessary. Moreover, for each partition
\lambda
, the
(q,t)
-enumeration
A_{\lambda}(q,t)
appears to be
Schur positive
in the triangular case
[
BM23
, Conjecture 1]
which would relate this combinatorial exploration to representation theory and the study of coinvariant spaces.

In terms of combinatorics, this raises two (very) difficult questions: finding an elementary, combinatorial proof of the
(q,t)
-symmetry for the enumeration of triangular Dyck paths and proving that they are Schur positive. This includes the classical and rational Dyck path cases, which are special cases of triangular Dyck paths. Besides, another challenge arises related to the work of
[
BPR12
]
on Tamari and
m
-Tamari intervals. Indeed, it is conjectured that a certain
q,t,r
-enumeration of Tamari intervals is also Schur positive and related to trivariate harmonic polynomials. Working on triangular partition, the question is now: is there a generalization of the Tamari lattice that would give a similar symmetric, Schur positive
q,t,r
enumeration of intervals
A_{\lambda}(q,t,r)
?

These problems are the motivation of the current paper. Our main results are stated in Theorem
4.10
and Theorem
5.8
: we study the specific (limited) case of
2
-partitions and give a direct proof of the Schur positivity in
2
and
3
variables. In particular, we are able to connect the symmetric polynomials
A_{\lambda}
introduced by Bergeron to an enumeration of intervals in the
\nu
-Tamari lattices of
[
PV15
]
. Moreover, we introduce new combinatorial tools such as the
triangular
and
sim-sym
tableaux as well as the
deficit
statistic which explore the combinatorial interpretation of the
dinv
statistic. This led us to state Conjecture
3
which states that our combinatorial interpretation of
A_{\lambda}
in terms of intervals of the
\nu
-Tamari lattices could be extended to larger class of triangular partitions.

The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
recalls the basic notions related to partitions and the definition and main properties of triangular partitions from
[
BM23
]
. In Section
3
, we define and explore some new combinatorial tools on triangular partitions, namely the
triangular tableau
, the
deficit
statistic and the
sim-sym
tableaux. The main result of this section is Proposition
3.6
where we prove that the
deficit
statistic actually gives the
dinv
.

Theorem
4.10
is proved in Section
4
. It relies heavily on the previous section and combinatorial tools. In particular, we prove that Schur positive enumeration can be obtained in various combinatorial ways using the notion of sym-sim and row-regular tableaux.

In Section
5
, we explain how this result can be extended to
3
variables with two major contributions. First we express Conjecture
3
on the combinatorial interpretation of the
A_{\lambda}
polynomals using the
\nu
-Tamari lattices in certain cases. We prove this conjecture on
2
-partitions in Theorem
5.8
relying on carefully designed enumerations of Tamari intervals on one hand and semi standard Young tableaux on the other hand.

We share the SageMath code used to run some examples and computations on
[
Pon25
]
.

2.
Background

2.1.
Partitions, Young tableaux and Schur functions

Definition 2.1
.

A
partition
1
1

1

This is actually an integer partition. As there is no ambiguity in the paper, we simply call them partitions.

\lambda
of size
n
is a tuple
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\lambda_{k}>0
such that
|\lambda|:=\sum_{i}^{k}\lambda_{i}=n
. For convenience, we consider partitions ending with an infinite number of
0
-parts,
\lambda_{j}:=0
for
j>k
. A
k
-partition
is a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k^{\prime}})
with
k^{\prime}\leq k
. We say that a partition
\mu
is a
sub-partition
of another partition
\lambda
if
\mu_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}
for all
i
.

As usual, we represent partitions by their
Ferrers diagram
(French style) such that each value
\lambda_{i}
, from bottom to top, corresponds to a line of
i
square cells as shown on Figure
1
. We identify each cell by a tuple
(\ell,col)
where
\ell
is its line (starting at 0) while
col
is its column. This is consistent with the implementation of the Ferrers diagram in
SageMath
and thus with our demo worksheet
[
Pon25
]
. For example, on Figure
1
, the bottom left-most cell is
(0,0)
, while the ending cell of the second row is
(1,2)
.

Definition 2.2
.

A
Young tableau
on a partition
\lambda
is an integer filling of the Ferrers diagram of
\lambda
(we place a number in each cell). We consider a tableau
\theta
as a function from the cells of
\lambda
to
\mathbb{N}
and write
\theta(c)
the value of the cell
c
in
\theta
. Besides, we rite
\theta_{i}
the number of cells
c
such that
\theta(c)=i
.

A
semi-standard Young tableau
on a partition
\lambda
is a tableau where numbers are strictly increasing along each column and largely increasing along each row. The set of semi-standard Young Tableaux over
\lambda
is written
SSYT(
\lambda
)
.

A
standard Young tableau
is a tableau on a partition
\lambda
such that numbers are strictly increasing along each column and line.

We present examples of partitions and tableaux in Figures
1
and
2
.

Not semi-standard

Semi-standard

Figure 2
.
Some Young tableaux for the partition
(3,3)
.

Schur functions
are infinite series defined on an infinite alphabet
x_{1},x_{2},\dots
. They form a famous basis of symmetric functions
[
Mac95
]
. There exists many equivalent definitions, we use the following. For a partition
\lambda
, the Schur function
s_{\lambda}
is given by

(1)

s_{\lambda}:=\sum_{\theta\in\mathrm{SSYT}(\lambda)}\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}x_{i}^{\theta_{i}}

Each term of the sum is given by a semi-standard Young tableau of the partition. For example, the (middle) semi-standard Young tableau of Figure
2
gives the monomial
x_{1}x_{2}^{3}x_{4}^{2}
. As there are infinitely many semi-standard Young tableaux for a given partition, a Schur function has infinitely many terms. In practice, Schur functions can be developed on finite number of variables by setting all other variables to
0
. For example, we have

(2)

\displaystyle s_{3,1}(q,t)

\displaystyle=q^{3}t+q^{2}t^{2}+qt^{3},

(3)

\displaystyle s_{2,1}(q,t,r)

\displaystyle=q^{2}r+qr^{2}+q^{2}t+2\,qrt+r^{2}t+qt^{2}+rt^{2},

(4)

\displaystyle s_{1,1,1}(q,t,r)

\displaystyle=qtr.

These are called
Schur polynomials
. Figure
3
illustrates the correspondence with semi-standard Young tableaux.

s_{3,1}(q,t)

2.2.
Triangular partitions

Definition 2.3

(From
[
BM23
]
)
.

A
triangular partition

\lambda
is a partition such that there exist two real numbers
r
and
s
with
\lambda_{j}=\lfloor r-\frac{jr}{s}\rfloor
for all
j\leq s
, and
\lambda_{j}=0
otherwise.

We say that the line joining
(r,0)
and
(0,s)
is an
r
-
s
-line
. Then
\lambda
is the greatest partition lying under the
r
-
s
-line. We also say that this line
cuts off

\lambda
. See an example on Figure
4
.

v

Figure 4
.
Left: the triangular partition
(4,3,1)
with one line that cuts it off and its slope vector. Right: a non-triangular partition
(4,4)
.

When there exists an
r
-
s
-line cutting off
\lambda
with
r
and
s
integer values,
\lambda
is said to be a
rectangular
partition. It is further said to be
rational
when
r
and
s
are coprime. Later on, it will be important to characterize the
slope
of the line. In accordance with
[
BM23
]
, this can be done through a single parameter
v
which characterizes a
slope vector
.

Definition 2.4
.

The
slope vector
of an
r
-
s
-line is the vector orthogonal to the line and of coordinates
(v,1-v)
. For simplicity, we write “the slope
v
”. We say that
\lambda
admits a slope
v
if it is cut off by an
r
-
s
-line of slope vector
(v,1-v)
.

A triangular partition admits an infinite number of slope vectors which corresponds to certain open interval for the value of
v
. Bergeron and Mazin
[
BM23
]
give a simple way to compute the slope interval using the hooks of the partition.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
c
be a cell of
\lambda
, the
arm
of
c
, written
a(c)
, is the number of cells lying to the right of
c
in
\lambda
and the
leg
of
c
, written
\leg(c)
, is the number of cells lying above
c
in
\lambda
. Then

(5)

\displaystyle v^{-}(c,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\leg(c)}{a(c)+\leg(c)+1},

\displaystyle v^{+}(c,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\leg(c)+1}{a(c)+\leg(c)+1}.

Then
]v^{-}(c,\lambda),\penalty\ v^{+}(c,\lambda)[
is the
admissible interval
of the cell
c
. Besides, we write

(6)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{-}

\displaystyle:=\max_{c\in\lambda}(v^{-}(c,\lambda)),

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\min_{c\in\lambda}(v^{+}(c,\lambda)).

Bergeron and Mazin prove that a partition is triangular if and only if the intersection of all admissible intervals given by
]v_{\lambda}^{-},v_{\lambda}^{+}[
is not empty and this also characterizes all possible slopes for the partition.

Lemma 2.6

(Lemma 1.2 of
[
BM23
]
)
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition, then
\lambda
is triangular if and only if
v_{\lambda}^{-}<v_{\lambda}^{+}
. Then for all
v\in]v_{\lambda}^{-},v_{\lambda}^{+}[
,
\lambda
is cut off by a line of slope vector
v
.

For example, on the triangular partition of Figure
4
, for
c
the cell
(0,0)
, we have
\leg(c)=2
,
a(c)=3
which gives
v^{-}(c,\lambda)=\frac{1}{3}
and
v^{+}(c,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}
. By computing the values for the other cells, we actually find that
v_{\lambda}^{-}=\frac{1}{3}
and
v_{\lambda}^{+}=\frac{1}{2}
. As
\frac{1}{3}<\frac{1}{2}
this is indeed a triangular partition. On the other hand, for the second partition of Figure
4
, for
c=(0,3)
the last cell of the lowest row, we find
a(c)=0
,
\leg(c)=1
and
v^{-}(c,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2}
whereas for
c^{\prime}=(1,0)
,the first cell of the second row, we have
\leg(c^{\prime})=0
,
a(c^{\prime})=3
, and
v^{+}(c^{\prime},\lambda)=\frac{1}{4}
. This is not a triangular partition.

In
[
Pon25
]
, we provide the code to generate all triangular partitions of a given size and to test if a given partition is triangular.

2.3.
Area, sim and (q,t)-enumeration

In this paper, we are interested in the
(q,t)
enumeration of sub-partitions of a given triangular partition
\lambda
. Following the terminology of
[
BM23
]
, we call such objects
triangular Dyck paths
.

Definition 2.7
.

A
triangular Dyck path
is a tuple
(\lambda,\mu)
such that
\mu
is a sub-partition of
\lambda
and
\lambda
is a triangular partition. The
area
of a triangular Dyck path is the number of cells belonging to
\lambda
but not
\mu
, given by
\area_{\lambda}(\mu):=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}
.

Note that we do not require that
\mu
is also triangular. We show an example on Figure
5
where
\lambda=(7,6,4,3,1)
and
\mu=(5,5,3,2)
. We draw the path between the two partitions in thick red. The area is made of cells in
\lambda\setminus\mu
,
i.e.
, the cells which are outside the path (in light red in the example). Here we have
\area_{\lambda}(\mu)=6
.

When
\lambda
is the staircase partition,
i.e.
,
\lambda=(n-1,n-2,\dots,1)
, then the triangular Dyck paths
(\lambda,\mu)
correspond to classical Dyck paths of length
n
: start with an up-step then read the path from top to bottom transforming each vertical step into an up-step and each horizontal step with a down-step, and finish with a down-step. See Figure
6
for an example. Similarly, when
\lambda
is a rational partition, then its sub-partitions are known as rational Dyck paths.

Figure 5
.
Left: triangular Dyck path
(7,6,4,3,1),(5,5,3,2)
with
{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)=6}
(cells in light red),
\ssim_{\lambda}(\mu)=13
(cells in light green), and
\ddef_{\lambda}(\mu)=2
(dotted yellow cells). Right: the same triangular Dyck path where the hook of a given cell is shown.

area, sim

3,0

2,1

1,2

1,1

0,3

regular Dyck paths

Figure 6
.
The 5 triangular Dyck paths of the partition
(2,1)
with their area and sim.

Definition 2.8

(From
[
BM23
]
, Section 4.1)
.

Let
(\lambda,\mu)
be a triangular Dyck path. A cell
c
of
\mu
is said to be
similar
if

(7)

v^{-}(c,\mu)<\frac{v_{\lambda}^{-}+v_{\lambda}^{+}}{2}\leq v^{+}(c,\mu).

In other words, the
mean slope
of the slope interval of
\lambda
belongs to the semi-closed admissible interval of the cell
c
in
\mu
. Then
\ssim_{\lambda}(\mu)
is the number of similar cells in the triangular Dyck path
(\lambda,\mu)
.

Remark 2.9
.

Note that in
[
BM23
]
, the large and strict inequalities are reversed. Both choices are arbitrary and give similar results (as long as there is one large and one strick inequality). In this this paper, placing the large inequality in second position allows for a better compatibility later with the Tamari lattices.

For example on the right of Figure
5
, we have selected a cell whose arm is
a(c)=3
and leg is
\ell(c)=2
. The mean slope of
\lambda
,
\frac{45}{112}
belongs to the admissible interval of the selected cell as we obtain
v^{-}(c,\mu)=\frac{1}{3}
and
v^{+}(c,\mu)=\frac{1}{2}
(from definitions (
5
)). By checking all cells, we obtain
\ssim_{\lambda}(\mu)=13
: the similar cells are colored in light green while the non similar cells are in dotted yellow. In
[
Pon25
]
you find an implementation of triangular Dyck paths with the computation of the
sim
statistic.

It is important to understand that the similarity of a cell depends on both partitions of the triangular Dyck path. Indeed, the mean slope is computed on
\lambda
but the admissible cell interval is computed on
\mu
. If
(\lambda,\mu)
is a triangular Dyck path such that all the cells of
\mu
are similar, then in particular
\mu
is triangular: a slight perturbation of the mean slope of
\lambda
will always cut off
\mu
(see
[
BM23
, Lemma 4.1]
).
When all cells of
\mu
are similar cells, we say that
(\lambda,\mu)
is a
mean-similar
triangular Dyck path.

The
sim
statistic already appears in
[
BHM+23
]
and is a generalization of the famous
dinv
statistic that can be found in
[
Hag08
]
for the classical
(q,t)
-Catalan enumeration. Indeed, when
\lambda
is the staircase partition, then the
sim
of
(\lambda,\mu)
is actually the
dinv
of the corresponding Dyck path as you can check on Figure
6
.

We can now define the
(q,t)
-polynomials associated to a triangular partition
\lambda
as:

(8)

A_{\lambda}(q,t)=\sum_{\mu\subseteq\lambda}q^{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\lambda}(\mu)}.

These polynomials have been shown to be symmetric in
q
and
t
via an algebraic proof
[
BHM+23
]
. However, the question of finding a combinatorial proof is still open even in the classical case. For example, for the partition
\lambda=(3,2)
whose triangular Dyck paths are shown on Figure
7
, we have the following:

(9)

A_{3,2}(q,t)=q^{5}+q^{4}t+q^{3}t^{2}+q^{2}t^{3}+qt^{4}+t^{5}+q^{3}t+q^{2}t^{2}+qt^{3}

area, sim

5,0

4,1

3,2

2,2

3,1

area, sim

2,3

1,4

1,3

0,5

Figure 7
.
The triangular Dyck paths of
\lambda=(3,2)
.

As the
A_{\lambda}(q,t)
polynomials are symmetric, they can be expressed in terms of Schur functions with the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1

(Conjecture 1 of
[
BM23
]
)
.

The polynomials
A_{\lambda}(q,t)
are Schur-positive.

Here are some examples of computation, more can be found in
[
BM23
]
or computed using our demo worksheet
[
Pon25
]
where we check the conjecture for triangular partitions up to size
10
.

(10)

\displaystyle A_{3,2,1}(q,t)

\displaystyle=s_{6}(q,t)+s_{4,1}(q,t)+s_{3,1}(q,t),

(11)

\displaystyle A_{3,2}(q,t)

\displaystyle=s_{5}(q,t)+s_{3,1}(q,t),

(12)

\displaystyle A_{2,2,1}(q,t)

\displaystyle=s_{5}(q,t)+s_{3,1}(q,t)

3.
Combinatorial interpretation of similar cells

In this section, we propose a new combinatorial interpretation for similar cells which is easier to use and leads to new results. The first step is to define a certain Young tableau associated to each triangular partition.

3.1.
Triangular Tableau and deficit

Bergeron and Mazin define the
ray
of a given slope vector
(v,1-v)
by the set of triangular partitions which admits
(v,1-v)
as a slope vector
[
BM23
, Formula (3.1)]
. They give the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.1

(Lemma 4.1 of
[
BM23
]
)
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition, then there exists
\epsilon>0
irrational, such that the set of mean-similar triangular Dyck paths
(\lambda,\mu)
are exactly those that lie on the ray corresponding to the slope
t_{\lambda}+\epsilon
.

Besides, they prove that the triangular partitions of a given ray form an infinite chain in the Young lattice restricted to triangular partitions. In particular, if
v
is an
irrational slope
and
R_{v}
its ray, then for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a unique triangular partition of size
n
in
R_{v}
and it is obtained by adding a well chosen box on the unique triangular partition
\mu
of size
n-1
in
R_{v}
. As a direct consequence, we obtain

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition. Then for all
0\leq k\leq|\lambda|
, there exists a unique mean-similar triangular Dyck path
(\lambda,\mu)
of area
k
. Besides, if
(\lambda,\alpha)
and
(\lambda,\mu)
are two mean-similar triangular Dyck paths with
|\alpha|\leq|\mu|
, then
\alpha\subseteq\mu
.

In other words, if
(\lambda,\mu)
is such that all cells of
\mu
are similar cells, then there is a unique way to remove a cell from
\mu
such that all remaining cells stay similar. We illustrate this on Figure
8
. Geometrically, the mean-slope of
\lambda
can always be slightly shifted to obtain an irrational slope. By
moving
this irrational slope towards the origin, we “touch” the cells of
\lambda
one by one, which gives the removal order on the cells of the sub-partitions in order to stay mean-similar, see Figure
9
for an example. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 3.3
.

The
Triangular Young tableau
of a triangular partition
\lambda
with
|\lambda|=n
is the unique standard Young tableau such that for all
0\leq k\leq n
, the cells
1
to
k
form a sub-partition
\mu
with
(\lambda,\mu)
a mean-similar triangular Dyck path.

\begin{array}[t]{*{3}c}\cline{1-2}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$5$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$4$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\end{array}

Figure 8
.
The mean-similar triangular Dyck paths of
\lambda=(3,2)
and the corresponding triangular Young tableau.

with its slope construction.

3.2.
Deficit of a sub-partition

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(\lambda,\mu)
be a triangular Dyck path and
\theta
a standard Young tableau of shape
\lambda
. We say that there is a
\theta
-inversion
in
(\lambda,\mu)
if there exists a cell
c_{1}=(i_{1},j_{1})
in
\mu
and a cell
c_{2}=(i_{2},j_{2})
in
\lambda\setminus\mu
such that
\theta(c_{1})>\theta(c_{2})
. In particular,
c_{1}
cannot be in the same line or column than
c_{2}
. Then we say that that the cell
c=(\min(i_{1},i_{2}),\min(j_{1},j_{2}))
, which is at the hook of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, is a
\theta
-deficit cell
and we call
deficit
of the triangular Dyck path
(\lambda,\mu)
with tableau
\theta
, written
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)
, the number of
\theta
-deficit cells.

1
2
4
7
3
5
8
6

Figure 10
.
A triangular dyck path on
(4,3,1)
with two deficit cell: 1 and 2.

See Figure
10
for an example:
1
is a deficit cell because it is at the hook of
6
and
4
and
2
is a deficit cell because it is at the hook of
5
and
7
. Note that it can happen that multiple
\theta
-inversions give the same deficit cell. Given their definition,
\theta
-deficit cells have some clear combinatorial properties, we express one of them in the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
c
be a
\theta
-deficit cell, then
c
is in the
interior
of the partition
\lambda
,
i.e.
,
a(c)>0
and
\leg(c)>0
.

Proof.

The cell
c
lies at the hook of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
. Besides
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
cannot be on the same line or column, which means
c_{1}\neq c
and
c_{2}\neq c
: the cell
c
must have a positive number of cells lying above it and to its right.
∎

We prove the following which gives a more combinatorial approach to the computation of similar cells.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition and
\theta
its triangular Young tableau as in Definition
3.3
. Then for any triangular Dyck path
(\lambda,\mu)
, the
\theta
-deficit cells of
(\lambda,\mu)
are exactly the non-similar cells of
\mu
. In particular, we have
|\lambda|=\area_{\lambda}(\mu)+\ssim_{\lambda}(\mu)+\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)
.

Proof.

We first show that non-
\theta
-deficit cells of
(\lambda,\mu)
are similar cells of
(\lambda,\mu)
.

Let
d
be a non-
\theta
-deficit cell. We choose
c
in the hook of
d
in
\mu
such that
\theta(c)
is maximal. As
d
is not a deficit, all cell
c^{\prime}
in the hook of
d
in
\lambda\backslash\mu
are such that
\theta(c^{\prime})>\theta(c)
. We now consider the mean-similar partition
\alpha
of size
|\alpha|=\theta(c)
,
i.e.
, the cells of
\alpha
are all the cells
x
of
\lambda
such that
\theta(x)\leq\theta(c)
. In particular, all the cells of the hook of
d
in
\mu
are also in
\alpha
whereas all the cells of of the hook of
d
in
\lambda\backslash\mu
are not: the hook of d in
\alpha
is the same than the hook of d in
\mu
. Furthermore,
d
is a similar cell in
(\lambda,\alpha)
by definition of a mean-similar partition, therefore
d
is similar in
(\lambda,\mu)
.

Let us now take
d
a
\theta
-deficit cell. Without loss of generality, we can consider that there is
c\in\mu
in the arm of
d
and
c^{\prime}\in\lambda\backslash\mu
in the leg of
d
such that
\theta(c)>\theta(c^{\prime})
(we can take the
c
as the last cell in the arm of
d
in
\mu
).

If we consider
\alpha
the mean-similar partition of size
\theta(c)
, the hook of
d
in
\mu
is striclty contained in the hook of
d
in
\alpha
(
c^{\prime}
is in the hook of
d
in
\alpha
but not in
\mu
). We write
a
the length of the arm of
c
in
\alpha
and
l
the length of its leg. Because
\alpha\backslash c
is still mean-similar, we have that
c
in
\alpha\backslash c
is similar. As such, we have:

\frac{l}{(a-1)+l+1}<\frac{v_{\lambda}^{-}+v_{\lambda}^{+}}{2}+\epsilon<\frac{l+1}{(a-1)+l+1}

However, in
\mu
, we have
l_{\mu}(d)<l
and
a_{\mu}(d)=a
(and as such
\frac{l_{\mu}(d)+1}{a_{\mu}(d)+l_{\mu}(d)+1}\leq\frac{l}{a+l}
). Therefore, we have:

\frac{l_{\mu}(d)}{a_{\mu}(d)+l_{\mu}(d)+1}<\frac{l_{\mu}(d)+1}{a_{\mu}(d)+l_{\mu}(d)+1}<\frac{v_{\lambda}^{-}+v_{\lambda}^{+}}{2}+\epsilon

This means that
d
is not similar.
∎

In the rest of the paper, we write
\ddef_{\lambda}(\mu):=\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)
of a triangular Dyck path where
\theta
is the triangular Young tableau.

For the staircase partition (which gives classical Dyck paths), the triangular tableau is the
top-down
tableau where we label the corners of the partition in decreasing order from top to bottom as shown in Figure
11
. The deficit then gives a new interpretation for the classical
dinv
statistic.

and

(

is a deficit cell). On the right, we have
\mu=(1,1,1)
and
\ssim_{\lambda}(\mu)=1
(
1
and

are deficit cells).

.
The triangular Dyck paths of the partition
(3,2)
with their
\theta
-similar cells for the two possible sym-sim tableaux.

The deficit statistic is defined for any tableau
\theta
and just happens to coincide with the non-similar cells when
\theta
is the triangular Young tableau of
\lambda
. It is then natural to extend the definition of the
sim
statistic to any Young tableau.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\theta
be a standard Young tableau of triangular shape
\lambda
. Then for any triangular Dyck paths
(\lambda,\mu)
, we say that a cell of
\mu
is
\theta
-similar
if it is not a
\theta
-deficit cell. We write
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)
the number of
\theta
-similar cells.

With that definition, again it is natural to extend the definition of (
8
) to this new setting, we define

(13)

A_{\theta}(q,t)=\sum_{\mu\subseteq\lambda}q^{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)}

where
\theta
is a standard Young tableau of shape
\lambda
. These polynomials are not symmetric in general. On the other hand, the triangular Young tableau is not the only tableau giving the symmetry. This led us to introduce the following objects which we believe are of high interest when studying the
(q,t)
symmetry.

Definition 3.8
.

A
sim-sym
(similar-symmetric) tableau is a Young tableau
\theta
of triangular shape
\lambda
such that
A_{\theta}(q,t)=A_{\lambda}(q,t)
.

Note that, in theory we could have
A_{\theta}(q,t)\neq A_{\lambda}(q,t)
and still be symmetric. From experimentation, we conjecture that it never happens.

It is important to note that this equality does not necessarily mean that
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=\ssim_{\lambda}(\mu)
for all triangular Dyck paths, only that we have a bijection over the triangular Dyck paths of
\lambda
which keeps the
area
constant while sending the
\theta
-
sim
statistic to the classical
sim
. Figure
12
gives the two possible sym-sim tableaux for the partition
(3,2)
along with the list of triangular Dyck paths with their
\theta
-similar cells. Other tableaux of
(3,2)
are not sim-sym. For example, for the following tableau

\begin{array}[t]{*{3}c}\cline{1-2}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$4$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$5$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\end{array}

we get, the polynomial

q^{5}+q^{4}t+q^{3}t^{2}+q^{2}t^{3}+qt^{4}+t^{5}+q^{3}t+q^{2}t^{2}+qt^{2}

which is not symmetric. The number of sim-sym tableaux seem to be rather small compared to the total number of tableaux. For example, for the partition
(4,2,1)
, we find
7
sim-sym tableaux (see Figure
13
) among the
35
standard Young tableaux.

\cline{1-4}\cr\end{array}

\begin{array}[t]{*{4}c}\cline{1-1}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$7$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$5$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$8$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$4$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$6$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\end{array}

Figure 13
.
The sim-sym tableaux of
(4,3,2)

Characterizing sim-sym tableaux appears to be an interesting but difficult question. One way to find different sim-sym tableaux is to choose a different slope to sweep down the partition. Indeed, the triangular Young tableau is defined using a slight deformation of the mean slope (to ensure that it is irrational). But any irrational slope would actually give a tableau and computational exploration suggests that they are always sim-sym tableaux. Nevertheless, they are not the only examples. For example, for the partition
(4,2,1)
, we find only two slope-tableaux which are the first two tableaux of Figure
13
(the first one is the mean-slope triangular tableau), but there are actually 7 sim-sym tableaux. In the rest of the paper, we characterize sim-sym tableaux on
2
-partitions and explore some interesting conjectures related to the lattice structure of triangular Dyck-paths and sim-sym tableaux.

All examples have been computed using
SageMath
and can be found in our demo worksheet
[
Pon25
]
.

4.
Sim-sym tableaux and
(q,t)
enumeration on
2
-partitions

Proving Conjecture
1
in general is difficult. In this section, we restrict our study to the case of
2
-partitions (partitions with only
2
parts).

4.1.
Characterization of triangular
2
-partitions

We start with a simple property of
2
-partitions.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a 2-partition,
\lambda
is triangular if and only if
n\leq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
.

Remark 4.2
.

By simple computation, the condition
n\leq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
is equivalent to
n\leq m-n+1
.

To prove the proposition, we use the following Lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a partition of length
2
. Then

(14)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m-n+2},

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{+}

\displaystyle=\min\left(\frac{2}{m+1},\frac{1}{n}\right).

Proof.

The maximal value of
v^{-}(c,\lambda)
is obtained for
c=(0,n-1)
the
n^{th}
cell of the first row. Indeed, for all cells
c^{\prime}
on the second row or right of
c
, we have
\leg(c^{\prime})=0
and so
v^{-}(c^{\prime},\lambda)=0
and then
c
is the cell maximizing the value
a(c)+\leg(c)+1
. We have
a(c)=m-n
and
\leg(c)=1
, hence the result.

The minimal value of
v^{+}(c,\lambda)
is obtained either on
c=(0,0)
, the first cell of the first row, or on
c=(1,0)
, the first cell of the second row. We get
a(0,0)=m-1
,
\leg(0,0)=1
,
a(1,0)=n-1
, and
\leg(1,0)=0
. This gives the result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

We use the characterization of triangular partitions given in Lemma
2.6
from Bergeron and Mazin.

If
n>\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
, in particular
n>\frac{m+1}{2}
and
v_{\lambda}^{+}=\frac{1}{n}
. As
m=\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor+\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
we find
m-n<\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor\leq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil<n
which gives
m-n+2\leq n
and so
v_{\lambda}^{+}\leq v_{\lambda}^{-}
: the partition is not triangular.

On the other hand, if
n\leq\frac{m}{2}
, we have
m-n+2\geq\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor+2
. And as we have
\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor\geq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil-1
as well as
\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor\geq\frac{m+1}{2}-1
. We obtain
m-n+2\geq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil+1>n
and
m+n+2>\frac{m+1}{2}
which gives
v_{\lambda}^{-}<v_{\lambda}^{+}
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

The result can also be seen geometrically from the original definition of triangular partition.
If
\lambda=(m,n)
is triangular, then there is a line that cuts off
\lambda
. This line necessarily passes strictly under the points
(1,3)
, and
(m+1,1)
. It then passes under the middle point
(\frac{m}{2}+1,2)
which gives
n\leq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
. Besides, by slightly translating the line passing through
(1,3)
and
(m+1,1)
and rotating it, we see that we can obtain all the wanted partitions.

4.2.
Row-regulars tableaux

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition. We say that a tableau
\theta
on
\lambda
is
row-regular
or, more precisely that it is the
i
-row-regular tableau of
\lambda
, if the labels on the upper line of
\lambda
are given by
n+i,n+i+2,n+i+4,\dots,n+i+2(n-1)
with
1\leq i\leq m-2(n-1)
.

As
\lambda
only has two (increasing) rows, this entirely characterizes
\theta
.

The
minimal
row-regular tableau of
\lambda
is the row-regular tableau with
i=1
, the
maximal
is the row-regular with
i=m-2(n-1)
and we call the row-regular tableau with
i=m-2n-3
(when it exists) the
pre-maximal
row-regular tableau of
\lambda
.

In particular, there are
m-2(n-1)
row-regulars tableaux for
\lambda=(m,n)
and the values
1,2,\dots,n
are always placed on the first row. On Figure
14
, we present all row-regulars tableaux of the partition
(9,4)
. The values of the upper row increase two by two, forcing a certain “alternation” between the two rows. If
\theta
is not the maximal row-regular tableau, the values of the lower row are always
1,2,\dots,n+i-1
, then
n+i+1,n+i+3,\dots,n+i+2(n-1)+1
, then all remaining values up to
n+m
.

\begin{array}[t]{*{9}c}\cline{1-4}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$5$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$7$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$9$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$11$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\

\cline{1-9}\cr\end{array}

\begin{array}[t]{*{9}c}\cline{1-4}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$7$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$9$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$11$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$13$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\

\cline{1-9}\cr\end{array}

minimal

pre-maximal

maximal

Figure 14
.
The
3
possible row-regulars tableaux of the partition
(9,4)
.

Lemma 4.6
.

The maximal row-regular tableau is the only one where the label
m+n
is placed on the upper row.

Proof.

This is direct from the definition. The maximal label of the upper row is given by
\ell=n+i+2(n-1)
. If
\theta
is the maximal row-regular tableau, then
i=m-2(n-1)
and
\ell=n+m
. If
\theta
is not the maximal row-regular tableau, then
i<m-2(n-1)
and
\ell<n+m
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mu=(m,n)
be a triangular
2
-partition and
\theta
an
i
-row-regular tableau on
\mu
. If
\lambda
is the partition
(m+k,n)
with
k>0
and
\theta^{\prime}
the tableau extending
\theta
on
\lambda
by labeling the extra cells with
m+n+1,m+n+2,\dots m+n+k
, then
\lambda
is also a triangular partition and
\theta^{\prime}
is an
i
-row-regular tableau on
\lambda
.

We say that
\lambda
is the extension of
\mu
by
k
cells.

Proof.

This is direct by definition. As
\mu
is triangular, we have
n\leq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil\leq\lceil\frac{m+k}{2}\rceil
so
\lambda
is triangular. The labels of the upper row are not changed and the condition on
i
is still satisfied as we have
1\leq i\leq m-2(n-1)\leq m+k-2(n-1)
.
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition, then the triangular Young tableau of
\lambda
is a row-regular tableau:

•

if
n=\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
, it is the maximal row-regular tableau;

•

if
n<\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
, it is the pre-maximal row-regular tableau.

In other words, the maximal value of the tableau is placed on either one of the two rows depending on the length
n
, then the values alternate between the two rows until the shortest row is filled. For the partition
(9,4)
of Figure
14
, as
4<\lceil\frac{9}{2}\rceil
, the triangular tableau is the pre-maximal row-regular tableau. We show some other examples on Figure
15
.

\begin{array}[t]{*{7}c}\cline{1-2}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$6$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$8$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-7}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$4$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$5$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$7$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$9$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\

.
The triangular tableaux of
(7,2)
,
(3,2)
and
(4,2)
.

v_{\lambda}^{-}=\frac{1}{4}

v_{\lambda}^{+}=\frac{1}{3}

v_{\lambda}^{-}=\frac{1}{5}

v^{-}(c,\mu)=\frac{1}{3}>\frac{7}{24}

Figure 16
.
Examples illustrating the proof of Proposition
4.8

Row-regular tableaux are somehow a generalization of the triangular tableau. We prove later that they are sim-sym tableaux and, in most cases, the only possible sim-sym tableaux on triangular
2
-partitions. First we show a useful property generalizing in a sense Definition
3.3
: one can “remove” the cells of a row-regular tableau one by one by keeping the sub-partition triangular.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular
2
-partition and
\theta
the
i
-row-regular tableau on
\lambda
. Then for
n+i\leq k\leq m+n
, the sub-partition
\mu
formed by the cells of
\lambda
with labels smaller than or equal to
k
is a triangular
2
-partition and the restriction of
\theta
to
\mu
is a row-regular tableau of
\mu
.

Proof.

Let us first prove the case where
k\in\{n+i,n+i+2,\dots,n+i+2(n-1)\}
. In other words,
k=n+i+2\ell
with
\ell\leq n-1
. Then
\mu
is equal to
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with
m^{\prime}=n+i-1+\ell
and
n^{\prime}=\ell+1
. We have
m^{\prime}-n^{\prime}+1=n+i-1\geq n
and
n^{\prime}\leq n
which proves that
\mu
is triangular. Now the labels on the upper row of
\mu
are given by
n+i,n+i+2,\dots,n+i+2\ell
which can be rewritten as
n^{\prime}+j,n^{\prime}+j+2,\dots,n^{\prime}+j+2(n^{\prime}-1)
with
j=n-n^{\prime}+i
. As
i\geq 1
and
n\geq n^{\prime}
, we have
j\geq 1
. Besides, we have
m^{\prime}-2(n^{\prime}-1)=n+i-1-\ell=n-n^{\prime}+i=j
. So the restriction of
\theta
to
\mu
is the
j
-row-regular tableau on
\mu
.

The other cases can be obtained by Lemma
4.7
. Indeed, if
k=n+i+2\ell+1
with
\ell\leq n-2
, we have
\mu=(n+i+\ell,\ell+1)
which is the extension of
(n+i+\ell-1,\ell+1)
by one cell, which was proven before. If
k\geq n+i+2(n-1)
, then we have
\mu=(k-n,n)
which is an extension of
(i+2(n-1),n)
, which was proven before (case
\ell=n-1
).
∎

4.3.
Explicit
(q,t)
-enumeration and Schur positivity

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem on row-regular tableaux.

Theorem 4.10
.

For
\lambda=(m,n)
a triangular partition and
\theta
a row-regular tableau, then

(15)

A_{\theta}(q,t)=\sum_{0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)}s_{m+n-2d,d}(q,t).

Remark 4.11
.

Note that for all
0\leq d\leq n
, then
m+n-2d\geq m-n
. So for all
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
, we have that
(m+n-2d,d)
is indeed a partition.

This proves in particular that all row-regular tableaux are sim-sym tableaux. Besides, as we have proved in Proposition
4.8
that the triangular tableau is a row-regular tableau, we obtain the following.

Corollary 4.12
.

Conjecture
1
is satisfied on
2
-partitions and for
\lambda=(m,n)
, then

(16)

A_{\lambda}(q,t)=\sum_{0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)}s_{m+n-2d,d}(q,t).

This last result was already stated in
[
BM23
]
and the formula was actually obtained by
[
GHSR20
, Theorem 3.1]
which explores a similar
(q,t)
-enumeration in a different combinatorial context.

Note that because
\lambda
is a triangular partition, we always have
n\leq\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil
, so
\min(n,m-n)=n
in all cases except when
m
is odd and
n=\frac{m+1}{2}
. For example, on the partition
\lambda=(9,4)
, we get

(17)

A_{\lambda}(q,t)=s_{13}+s_{11,1}+s_{9,2}+s_{7,3}+s_{5,4}.

Before we prove the theorem, let us notice a few facts. Using the definition of Schur functions given in (
1
), remember that

(18)

s_{b,a}=q^{b}t^{a}+q^{b-1}t^{a+1}+\dots+q^{a}t^{b}

with
a\leq b
. Indeed, these are all the ways to fill a
2
-partitions with values
1
and
2
as a semi-standard Young tableau: the first
a
cells of the lower row are filled with
1
, the second row is filled with
2
and the
b-a
remaining cells of the lower row are filled with a series of
1
(possibly empty) followed by a series of
2
(possibly empty). In particular, the degree is always
a+b
.

In particular, all summands of
s_{b,a}
are distinct. Besides, the Schur functions summed in (
15
) are all of different degrees, so all
q^{i}t^{j}
summands of (
15
) are disctincts. In fact, each Schur function in the sum corresponds to a given deficit
d
and sums the elements
q^{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)}
for all triangular Dyck paths of deficit
d
.

It follows that the theorem is the direct consequence of the following result.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition and
\theta
a row-regular tableau on
\lambda
. Then, for each
d
such that
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
, and each
a
, such that
d\leq a\leq m+n-2d
, there is a unique triangular Dyck path
(\lambda,\mu)
with
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)=d
and
\area_{\lambda}(\mu)=a
. These are the only possible triangular Dyck paths of
\lambda
.

We give a constructive proof. Remember that a triangular Dyck path is given by a subparition
\mu
of
\lambda
. It can be constructed by “removing” cells from
\lambda
.

Definition 4.14
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition,
\theta
the
i
-row-regular tableau on
\lambda
, then for
d
and
a
such that
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
and
d\leq a\leq m+n-2d
, we define
\psi_{d,a}(\lambda)
the triangular Dyck paths constructed as follows:

•

If
a=d
, then

–

Case 1: if the maximal label
m+n
is on the lower row, we remove
d
cells from the upper row.

–

Case 2: otherwise (the maximal label
m+n
is on the upper row), we remove
d
cells from the lower row.

•

If
d<a\leq m-d-i+2
, we first remove the
a-d
cells from
\lambda
with highest labels, then

–

Case 1: if the remaining cell with maximal label (
m+n-(a-d)
) is on the lower row, we remove
d
cells from the upper row.

–

Case 2: otherwise (the remaining maximal label
m+n-(a-d)
is on the upper row), we remove
d
cells from the lower row.

•

If
m-d-i+2<a\leq m+n-2d
, the triangular Dyck path is given by the subpartition
(m+n-a-d,d)
.

We illustrate this construction on an example in Figure
17
with the three row-regular tableaux of the partition
(9,4)
and all possible values of
a
for
d=2
. The first line corresponds to the
a=d
case. For the first two tableaux, the maximal label cell
13
is on the lower row, so the subparition has been obtained by removing two cells from the upper row. On the last tableau (which is the maximal row-regular tableau), the cell with label
13
is on the upper row, so we have removed two cells from the lower row.

Below the first line, we see an illustration of the
d<a\leq m-d-i+2
cases. We have written a temporary partition
\lambda^{\prime}
corresponding to the partition
\lambda
where the
a-d
maximal cells have been removed. Then
2
extra cells are removed from
\lambda^{\prime}
either on the lower or upper row, depending on the position of cell with label
m+n-(a-d)
. The last rows illustrate the
a>m-d-i+2
cases.

.
Illustration of the proof of Lemma
4.15
on the three row-regular tableaux of
\lambda=(9,4)
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition and
\theta
the
i
-row-regular tableau on
\lambda
. Then
\psi_{d,a}(\lambda)=(\lambda,\mu)
is well defined and we have
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)=d
and
\area_{\lambda}(\mu)=a
.

Proof.

We show the result on the different cases depending on the value of
a
.

Case
a=d

First note that for
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
, it is always possible to construct the subpartition
\mu
by removing
d
cells from upper / lower row as we have both
d\leq n
and
d\leq m-n
. Now notice that we have removed the cells from the row which did not contain the maximal value. We write
D:=\{n-d+1,n-d+2,\dots,n\}
and call the cells labeled by
D
, the
D
cells. In all cases, we obtain that the
D
cells are deficit cells.

Indeed, in Case 1, the removed cells are right above the
D
cells and are labeled with
n+i+2(n-d),n+i+2(n-d)+2,\dots,n+i+2(n-1)
with
i\geq 1
, whereas the cells labeled
n+i+2(n-d)+1,n+i+2(n-d)+3,\dots,n+i+2(n-1)+1
lie to the right of the
D
cells which create the deficit.

In Case 2, the removed cells are labeled
m+n-1-2(d-1),\dots,m+n-3,m+n-1
by definition of the maximal row-regular tableau. Besides, the cells right above the
D
cells are labeled
m+n-2(d-1),\dots,m+n-2,m+n
which create the deficit.

Case
d<a\leq m-d-i+2

We use Proposition
4.9
with
k=m+n-(d-a)
. In other other words, we look at the difference between
a
and
d
and remove this number of cells from
\lambda
(in reverse order of their label in
\theta
). We obtain a partition
\lambda^{\prime}=(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
which by Proposition
4.9
is still triangular and labeled by a row-regular tableau. Now if we can construct a triangular Dyck path
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu)
with both deficit and area equal to
d
, this gives a triangular Dyck path
(\lambda,\mu)
with deficit
d
and area
a
. This construction is only possible if
d\leq\min(n^{\prime},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime})
and can be done using the
a=d
case treated above on
\lambda^{\prime}
. It is illustrated on Figure
17
. See for example, the
a=4
example on the
1
-row-regular tableau. As
a-d=2
, we remove the two cells labeled
13
and
12
from
\lambda
and obtain
\lambda^{\prime}=(7,4)
. Now the maximal label is
11
and placed on the upper row, so we are in Case 2 and we remove two extra cells from the lower row. We obtain
\mu=(5,4)
. The deficit of both
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu)
and
(\lambda,\mu)
is
2
and the area of
(\lambda,\mu)
is
2+2=4
. We illustrate many other cases on Figure
17
.

We need to prove that
a\leq m+n-2d
implies that the condition
d\leq\min(n^{\prime},m^{\prime}-n^{\prime})
is satisfied. As
\theta
is an
i
-row-regular tableau, the label of the first cell of the upper row is
n+i
and the label of the
d^{th}
cell is
n+i+2(d-1)
. Now
a\leq m-d-i+2
implies that
n+i+2(d-1)\leq m+n-(a-d)
. In other words, the
d^{th}
cell of the upper row still belongs to
\lambda^{\prime}
which translates as
d\leq n^{\prime}
and proves that the construction is possible.

Case
m-d-i+2<a\leq m+n-2d

We take
\mu=(m+n-a-d,d)
and claim that the triangular Dyck path
(\lambda,\mu)
has the right properties. First note that
\mu
is well defined because
a\leq m+n-2d
by definition, which gives
m+n-a-d\leq d
. The area of
(\lambda,\mu)
is given by
n-d+m-(m+n-a-d)=a
. We need to prove that the deficit is
d
. The
d
cells of the upper line of
\mu
have labels
n+i,n+i+2,\dots,n+i+2(d-1)
. Beside, the first
n+i-1
cells of the lower row of
\lambda
are labeled by
1,2,\dots,n+i-1
by definition of the row-regular tableau. We want to show that the first
d
cells of the lower row of
(\lambda,\mu)
are deficit cells. We look at the cell with label
n+i-1
. As we have
a>m-n-i+2
, this gives that
n+i-1>m+n-d-a
, so the cell with label
n+i-1
is not contained in
\mu
. This implies that the following cells of the lower line
n+i+2-1,\dots,n+i+2(d-1)-1
are also not contained in
\mu
, whereas the first
d
cells of the upper row
n+i,n+i+2,\dots,n+i+2(d-1)
are: this proves that
(\lambda,\mu)
has deficit
d
. This is illustrated on Figure
17
: on the
1
-row-regular tableau, it corresponds to the
a=9
case, on the
2
-row-regular tableau, to the
a=8
and
a=9
cases, and on the
3
-row-regular tableau, to the
a=7
,
a=8
, and
a=9
cases.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.13
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition and
\theta
a row-regular tableau on
\lambda
.
By Lemma
4.15
, we know that for each
d
such that
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
, and each
a
, such that
d\leq a\leq m+n-2d
, the triangular Dyck path
\psi_{d,a}(\lambda)
has deficit
d
and area
a
. This implies in particular that for
(d^{\prime},a^{\prime})\neq(d,a)
, then
\psi_{d^{\prime},a^{\prime}}(\lambda)\neq\psi_{d,a}(\lambda)
, the application is injective. We can prove that it is also surjective using a simple counting argument.
Let
D
be the number of triangular Dyck paths obtained by the
\psi_{d,a}(\lambda)
construction and
S
the number of subpartitions of
\lambda
. If we prove that
D=S
, we are done.

The value
S
, can be easily computed. Let
\mu=(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
be a subpartition of
\lambda
. Then we have
0\leq n^{\prime}\leq n
and
n^{\prime}\leq m^{\prime}\leq m
which gives
m-n^{\prime}+1
possible subpartitions for each value
n^{\prime}
. We obtain

(19)

S=\sum_{n^{\prime}=0}^{n}m-n^{\prime}+1=\frac{(2m-n+2)(n+1)}{2}.

Now, for each
d
with
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
, we can construct
m+n-2d-d+1=m+n-3d+1
triangular Dyck paths and so we obtain

(20)

D=\sum_{d=0}^{\min(m,m-n)}m+n-3d+1.

If
\min(m-n,n)=n
, then we get

(21)

D=\frac{(m+n+1+m+n-3n+1)(n+1)}{2}=S.

If
\min(m-n,n)=m-n
, then we have

(22)

D=\frac{(-m+5n+2)(m-n+1)}{2}.

As we are working with triangular partitions, the only case where
\min(m-n,n)=m-n
is when
m
is odd and
n=\frac{m+1}{2}
. By simple substitution in
D
and
A
, we obtain that numerators in both are given by
\frac{3(m+1)(m+3)}{4}
and so
D=S
in all cases.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.10
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition and
\theta
a row-regular tableau on
\lambda
. Let
d
be such that
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
. Then the summand
s_{m+n-2d,d}(q,t)
is equal to

(23)

\sum_{\mu}q^{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)}

summed over the subpartition
\mu
of
\lambda
such that
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)=d
. Indeed, we have

(24)

s_{m+n-2d,d}(q,t)=\sum_{a=d}^{m+n-2d}q^{a}t^{m+n-d-a}.

By Proposition
4.13
, we know that for a fixed
d
, there is exactly one triangular Dyck path
\psi_{d,a}(\lambda)=(\lambda,\mu)
for each value
a
such that
d\leq a\leq m+n-2d
. Each of these triangular Dyck paths corresponds to a summand

(25)

q^{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)}=q^{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)}t^{m+n-a-\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)}=q^{a}t^{m+n-a-d}.

As these are the only possible triangular Dyck paths and
d
can only vary between
0
and
\min(n,m-n)
, we obtain the formula.
∎

4.4.
Sim-sym tableaux on 2-partitions

We have seen that row-regular tableaux are sim-sym. In most cases, this is actually an equivalence relation.

Theorem 4.16

(Characterization of sim-sym tableaux on
2
-partitions)
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition such that
n\neq 2
, then
\theta
is a sim-sym tableau if and only if it is a row-regular tableau.

If
n=2
, then
\theta
is a sim-sym tableau if and only if
\theta
is a row-regular tableau or the values on the upper line are
2
and
5
.

Figure 18
.
The 5 different sim-sym tableaux on (6,2).

Definition 4.17
.

For
\lambda
a partition,
\theta
a sim-sym tableau on
\lambda
and
\mu\subset\lambda
,
we write
a_{\theta}(\mu)(q,t)=q^{\area_{\lambda}(\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)}

For the next two lemmas, we consider
\lambda=(m,n)
a triangular partition and
\theta
a sim-sym tableau.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
\mu\subset\lambda
, then
\area_{\lambda}(\mu)\geq\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)\geq\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)

Proof.

Using the formula of
4.12
, as
\theta
is sim-sym, we have

(26)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}a_{\theta}(\mu)(q,t)

\displaystyle=A_{\lambda}(q,t)

(27)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)}s_{m+n-2d,d}(q,t)

Hence, there exist
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
and
d\leq a\leq m+n-2d
such that:

a_{\theta}(\mu)(q,t)=q^{a}t^{m+n-d-a}

As such,
\area_{\lambda}(\mu)=a\geq d
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m+n-d-a\geq m+n-d-(m+n-2d)=d

∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\mu,\tau
two subpartitions of
\lambda
such that
\area_{\lambda}(\mu)=\area_{\lambda}(\tau)
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=\ssim_{\theta}(\tau)
.
Then
\mu=\tau

Proof.

Let us consider such
\mu
and
\tau
. We have
a_{\theta}(\mu)(q,t)=a_{\theta}(\tau)(q,t)
.

However, each monomial in a Schur function
s_{i,j}(q,t)
only appears with multiplicity 1.
Furthermore, for any
d\neq d^{\prime}
,
deg(s_{m+n-2d,d})=m+n-d\neq deg(s_{m+n-2d^{\prime},d^{\prime}})
.
As such, no monomial of
s_{m+n-2d,d}
appears in
s_{m+n-2d^{\prime},d^{\prime}}
.
Hence,
a_{\theta}(\mu)(q,t)
appears with multiplicity 1 in
A_{\theta}(q,t)
and
\mu=\tau
.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

For any
1\leq l\leq|\lambda|
, there is a subpartition
\mu
of size
|\mu|=l
, such that
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)=1
.

Proof.

This is a direct corollary of Corollary
4.12
and Lemma
4.15
.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

If n = 2, and
\theta
is the standard young tableau with values 2 and 5 on the upper line, then
\theta
is sim-sym.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.10
, we know that the tableay
\theta^{\prime}
with values 3 and 5 on the upper line is sim-sym.
The only difference between
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
is swapping the values 2 and 3.
Swapping the value only changes the similarity of the first cell in the first line.

For any
\mu\subset\lambda
, if
(2,1)\subset\mu
then the first cell is a similar cell in both cases.
Otherwise, if
(2,1)\not\subset\mu
but
(3)\subset\mu
then the first cell is a deficit cell in both cases.
Finally, if
\mu=(1)
, the first cell is also a similar cell in both cases, and if
\mu=\emptyset
, it’s in the area.

As such, the only subpartition in which the swapping has an impact are
(2)
and
(1,1)
.
We can easily see that
\ssim_{\theta}((2))=\ssim_{\theta^{\prime}}((1,1))
and
\ssim_{\theta^{\prime}}((2))=\ssim_{\theta}((1,1))
.
As such
A_{\theta}(q,t)=A_{\theta^{\prime}}(q,t)
and we get that
\theta
is sim-sym.
∎

Lemma 4.22
.

For
\theta
a tableau on
\lambda
, if
\theta
is sim-sym, then
\theta
is row-regular or n=2 and the values on the upper line are 2 and 5.

Proof.

Let us suppose that
\theta
is a standard Young tableau on
\lambda
and is not row-regular. We show that
the only possibility is that
n=2
and the upper values are 2 and 5. All other cases lead to a contraction.

Indeed, there are 3 possible cases:

(1)

The biggest value on the upper is smaller or equal to
3n-2
;

(2)

there exists two adjacent cells on the lower line with values
k
and
k+i
with
i\geq 3
;

(3)

there exists two adjacent cells on the upper line with values
k
and
k+i
with
i\geq 3
.

Case 1

As
\lambda
is triangular, we have
m\geq 2n-1
. As such, we can consider
\mu=(2n-1)
.
As
\theta
is a standard Young tableau, the value in the n-th cell on the upper line is greater than the values of each cells to its left.
As such, there are n cells on the upper row with a value lower or equal to
3n-2

Hence there can be only
3n-2-n=2n-2
cells on the lower row with a value lower or equal to
3n-2
.
Hence the value on the
(2n-2)
-th cell is necessarely
3n-1
in this case.
Therefore, the first n cells in
\mu
are deficit cells (and are the only deficit cells).
So
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)=n
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=2n-1-\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)=n-1
. This is in contradiction with Lemma
4.18
.

Case 2

In this case, there are adjacent cells in the upper row with value
k+1
to
k+i-1
.

•

If the cell numbered
k+i-1
is above
k
, we can consider the following subpartition:

\mu
whose last cell in the lower line is valued
k+i
, and whith no cell in the upper line.

\mu
is of size at least 3, because there are at least
i\geq 3
cells above
\mu
(the cells numbered
k+1
to
k+i-1
and the one above
k+i
).
However,
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu)=|\mu|-1\geq 2\geq\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=1
, which is in contradiction with Lemma
4.18
.

•

Otherwise, the cell above
k
is greater than
k+1
.
We can consider the 3 following subpartition of size
k+1
:

–

\mu_{1}
whose last cell in the lower line is valued
k+i
, and whose last cell on the upper line is stopping short of the cell valued
k+1
.

–

\mu_{2}
whose last cell in the lower line is valued
k
, and whose last cell on the upper line is valued
k+1
.

–

And finally,
\mu_{3}
,whose last cell in the lower line is juste before the cell valued
k
, and whose last cell on the upper line is valued
k+2
.

We get
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu_{2})=0
,
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu_{1})=i-1\geq 2
and
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu_{3})=2
.
Furthermore, we can see than every other subparitions of size
k+1
has a deficit greater than
i-1
if it has more cell on the upper line than
\mu_{1}
or greater than 2 otherwise.
As such, there is no subpartition of size
k+1
and of def 1, which is in direct contradiction with Lemma
4.20
.

Case 3

In this case, there are adjacent cells in the lower line with value
k+1
,
k+2
,…,
k+i-1
.
We suppose we are not in any of the previous cases.
There are 3 possibilities:

•

if the cell numbered
k+i
is not above the cell
k+1
.
We can consider the 3 following subpartitions of size
k+1
:

–

\mu_{1}
whose last cell in the lower line is valued
k+2
, and whose last cell on the upper line is stopping short of the cell valued
k
.

–

\mu_{2}
whose last cell in the lower line is valued
k+1
, and whose last cell on the upper line is valued
k
.

–

And finally,
\mu_{3}
, whose last cell in the lower line is juste before the cell valued
k+1
, and whose last cell on the upper line is valued
k+i
.

We get
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu_{2})=0
, the cell in
\mu_{2}
are exactly the ones numbered 1 to
k+1
.
And
\ddef_{\theta}(\mu_{1})=1=\ddef_{\theta}(\mu_{3})
.
In the first subpartition, the only deficit cell is the cell under
k
, and in the third, the only deficit cell is the one under
k+i
.
This is in contradiction with Lemma
4.20
.

•

if the cell numbered
k+i
is above
k+1
and
i\geq 4
.
We use the same idea, but with 3 subpartitions of size
k+2
that each get one more cell in the lower row than their respective previous subpartition.
Hence, we also get a contradiction with Lemma
4.18
.

•

Finally, if the cell numbered
k+i
is above
k+1
and
i=3
.
We can consider that we are in the first instance of adjacent cells with consecutive numbers on the lower line, and that the other instance are of this nature.
Because there is not case 2 in
\theta
and we are considering the first instance of case 3, we can deduce that the cells before
k
are numbered
k-2
,
k-4
, …until the first cells of the upper line.
Likewise, the cells before
k+1
are numbered
k-1
,
k-3
, …until the second cell of the lower line (whose numbered is one greater than the number of the first cell in the upper line).
As such, the first and second cells in the lower line are numbered 1 and 3 and the first in the second line is numbered 2.
Because there are 2 subpartitions of size 3, one of the has to have deficit 0 and the other 1.

(2,1)
has deficit 0, hence
(3)
must have deficit
1
.
Because 1 is a deficit cell, 3 should not be a deficit cell, hence the number in the third cell in the lower line is smaller than the number of the second cell in the upper line.
As such, we get that the third cell is 4 and the second on the upper line is 5.
If there are any cells after 5, they would have to be numbered 7, then 9,…otherwise we would have the first subcase.
But that we give a contradiction with the case 1, hence there are only 2 cells on the upper line, and they are numbered 2 and 5, which gives us the special case.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.16
.

We just combine Lemma reflem:sim-sym-to-row-reg and Theorem
4.10
.
∎

5.
Lattice and interval enumeration

5.1.
Tamari lattice and motivation

The Tamari lattice
[
Tam51
]
is a well known lattice on Catalan objects, often defined on Dyck paths. It has many known generalizations such as the
m
-Tamari lattices
[
BPR12
]
and the
\nu
-Tamari lattices
[
PV15
]
. The
m
-Tamari lattices in particular have been introduced in the context of trivariate diagonal harmonic polynomials and are the subject of many interesting conjectures
[
PR12
]
.

More precisely, the polynomials
A_{\lambda}(q,t)
of (
8
) sometimes occur as the expansion in two variables of the characters of a certain
GL_{2}
-action. Hence, they are Schur positive, as the Schur functions are the characters of the irreducibles in this context. For all triangular partitions
\lambda
, one can define a symmetric function
A_{\lambda}
corresponding to the Hilbert series of alternating component of multivariate diagonal harmonic polynomials
[
Ber22
]
. For example, we have that

(28)

\displaystyle A_{2,1}

\displaystyle=s_{3}+s_{1,1},

(29)

\displaystyle A_{3,2,1}

\displaystyle=s_{6}+s_{4,1}+s_{3,1}+s_{1,1,1}.

When expanded on two variables, the term
s_{1,1,1}(q,t)
in
A_{3,2,1}
is
0
and we get (
10
), the
(q,t)
-enumeration of Dyck paths. If we expand the functions on three variables, we obtain
A_{2,1}(1,1,1)=13
and
A_{3,2,1}(1,1,1)=68
the number of intervals in the Tamari lattice of sizes
3
and
4
respectively. This is part of a larger conjecture on
A_{\lambda}
and the Tamari lattice related to trivariate diagonal harmonic polynomials. For
\lambda=(n-1,n-2,\dots,1)
the staircase partition, it is conjectured that
A_{\lambda}(q,t,r)
enumerates the intervals of the Tamari lattice of size
n
with
q
counting the
distance
between the two elements of the interval and
t
the
dinv
of the maximal element. For example, we have

(30)

A_{3,2,1}(q,1,1)=q^{6}+2q^{5}+5q^{4}+10q^{3}+15q^{2}+21q+14.

The distance of an interval
[d_{1},d_{2}]
is the length of the longest chain in the lattice between
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
. The constant term
14
correspond to the
14
elements with “distance 0”,
i.e.
, the 14 intervals
[d,d]
where
d
is a Dyck path of size
4
. The term
q^{6}
corresponds to the interval
[\hat{0},\hat{1}]
between the minimal and maximal elements in the lattice. The full expansion on
A_{2,1}
gives

(31)

A_{2,1}(q,t,r)=q^{3}+q^{2}t+qt^{2}+t^{3}+q^{2}r+qtr+t^{2}r+qr^{2}+tr^{2}+r^{3}+qt+qr+tr.

The combinatorial interpretation of the third parameter
r
is a difficult open question listed as Problem 5 in
[
PR12
]
.

5.2.
Conjecture on the
\nu
-Tamari lattice

The aforementioned conjectures and combinatorial interpretation naturally extend to
m
-Tamari lattices, where
\lambda=(m(n-1),m(n-2),\dots,m,0)
is an
m
-staircase partition. Dyck paths and
m
-Dyck paths can be understood as subpartitions of the staircase and
m
-staircase partitions respectively. It is then a natural question to explore possible combinatorial interpretations of the
A_{\lambda}
polynomials expanded on three variables for
\lambda
a triangular partition. This interpretation should take the form of an interval enumeration on a lattice structure involving triangular Dyck paths.

In
[
PV15
]
, Préville-Ratelle and Viennot introduce a generalization of the Tamari lattice, now mostly referred to as the
\nu
-Tamari lattice and largely studied
[
CPS16
,
FP17
]
. These lattices are defined on subparitions of
any
given partition. In particular, it can be defined on triangular Dyck paths of a given triangular partition
\lambda
. Below is the definition we use in this paper.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition and
(\mu,\lambda)
. We write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)
and
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots)
. We choose
j
such that
\mu_{j}>\mu_{j+1}
(the end of line
j
is a
corner
) and
v:=\lambda_{j}-\mu_{j}
. Let
i<j
be the minimal possible value, such that for all
i<k<j
,
\lambda_{k}-\mu_{k}>v
. Then the
rotation
of
\mu
at line
j
is the triangular Dyck path
(\alpha,\lambda)
such that:

•

\alpha_{k}=\mu_{k}
for all
k\leq i
and all
k>j
;

•

\alpha_{k}=\mu_{k}-1
for all
k
such that
i<k\leq j
.

We say that
(\alpha,\lambda)

covers

(\mu,\lambda)
. We define the
\nu
-Tamari poset on subparitions of
\lambda
as the partial order
obtained by transitive closure of the rotation operation. In other words,
(\mu,\lambda)\prec(\tau,\lambda)
if and only if
\tau
can be obtained from
\mu
by a series of rotations.

We show an example on Figure
19
. We basically choose a starting line and remove one box per line going down as long as the number of red boxes on the line is bigger than on the starting line. This coincides (up to some rotation) with the original definition of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice given in
[
PV15
]
, where
\nu
is the path drawn by the partition
\lambda
. In particular, this is a lattice : any two elements
\mu
and
\alpha
admit a
join
(or least upper bound) written
\mu\wedge\alpha
and a
meet
(or greatest lower bound) written
\mu\vee\alpha
. See an example of the complete lattice for
\lambda=(4,2,1)
on Figure
20
.

Figure 19
.
Example of
\nu
-Tamari rotations on subpartitions

Figure 20
.
The
\nu
-Tamari lattice for
\lambda=(4,2,1)

We write the following lemma which is a well known fact about the Tamari and
\nu
-Tamari lattices and will be useful later on. It only states that the
\nu
-Tamari lattice is an extension of the (reversed) order on partition inclusion. In paritcular, the minimal element is
(\lambda,\lambda)
and the maximal is
(\epsilon,\lambda)
where
\epsilon
is the empty partition.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition and
\mu
and
\alpha
two subpartitions such that
(\mu,\lambda)\preceq(\alpha,\lambda)
in the
\nu
-Tamari lattice of
\lambda
. Then
\alpha\subseteq\mu
as partitions,
i.e.
,
\alpha_{1}\leq\mu_{1},\alpha_{2}\leq\mu_{2},\dots
.

Proof.

Each cover relation removes at least one cell from the subpartition and never adds any.
∎

Remember that we are trying to find a combinatorial interpretation for some general
A_{\lambda}
symmetric polynomials, for which we do not have a general formula. Anyway, we can summarize some “good” necessary properties in the problem below.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition and
\theta
a sim-sym tableau on
\lambda
. Let
\preceq
be a partial order on the triangular Dyck paths of
\lambda
. We define two statistics on intervals of
\preceq
. Let
\tau\preceq\mu
be an interval, then

•

\dist(\tau,\mu)
is the length of the longest chain between
\tau
and
\mu
;

•

\ssim_{\theta}(\tau,\mu):=\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)
,
i.e.
, the
sim
of the interval is the
sim
(relatively to the tableau
\theta
) of the maximal element.

Problem 2
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition and
\theta
a sim-sym tableau on
\lambda
. Find a partial order
\preceq
on the triangular Dyck paths of
\lambda
such that the polynomial

(32)

\sum_{\tau\preceq\mu}q^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\tau,\mu)}

summed over the intervals of
\preceq
is symmetric and Schur positive.

Note that what we actually want is for the polynomial to be equal to some Schur positive function
A_{\lambda}
expanded in
(q,t,1)
. Let us start with a negative result.

Proposition 5.4
.

The
\nu
Tamari lattice does not provide a solution in general to Problem
2
.

Proof.

Let us first give a counter-example where
\theta
is fixed to be the triangular tableau. Let
\lambda=(4,2,1)
. The
\nu
-Tamari lattice is given as an example in Figure
20
. The triangular standard tableau is given on Figure
21
.

\begin{array}[t]{*{4}c}\cline{1-1}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{6}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-2}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{3}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{5}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{1}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{2}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{4}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{7}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\end{array}

Figure 21
.
The triangular tableau of
\lambda=(4,2,1)

If we compute the polynomial (
32
), we obtain

(33)

\displaystyle q^{7}+q^{6}t+q^{5}t^{2}+q^{4}t^{3}+q^{3}t^{4}+q^{2}t^{5}+qt^{6}+t^{7}+q^{6}+2q^{5}t+2q^{4}t^{2}+2q^{3}t^{3}

\displaystyle+2q^{2}t^{4}+3qt^{5}+t^{6}+2q^{5}+4q^{4}t+5q^{3}t^{2}+6q^{2}t^{3}+3qt^{4}+2t^{5}+3q^{4}+6q^{3}t

\displaystyle+6q^{2}t^{2}+6qt^{3}+3t^{4}+4q^{3}+8q^{2}t+8qt^{2}+4t^{3}+4q^{2}+7qt+4t^{2}+3q+3t+1

which is not symmetric.

Nevertheless, it is possible to find a tableau for which the polynomial is symmetric (see the top-down tableau of Figure
23
). One would like a counter example which fails on every tableau of
\lambda
. This is the case for
\lambda=(4,3,1)
. As the symmetry on intervals would imply the sim-area symmetry on triangular Dyck paths, one can test only sim-sym tableaux. There are four of them shown on Figure
22
. The computed polynomials on each of them is not symmetric. All computations can be found in
[
Pon25
]
.
∎

\begin{array}[t]{*{4}c}\cline{1-1}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$6$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\

\cline{1-4}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$4$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$6$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-4}\cr\end{array}

\begin{array}[t]{*{4}c}\cline{1-1}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$7$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-3}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$5$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$8$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\

\cline{1-4}\cr\end{array}

Figure 22
.
Sim-sym tableaux of
\lambda=(4,3,1)

Even though the
\nu
-Tamari lattice does not give a general solution to problem
2
, it is still interesting to look at as it gives a solution in
some
cases. The first observation is that the triangular tableau is not the natural choice for solving this problem. This is the reason we introduced the notion of sim-sym tableaux in the first place. Indeed, if
\lambda
is partition of
n
, then a longest chain on the
\nu
-Tamari lattice is of length
n
as it corresponds to removing one cell at a time. If we number these cells in reverse order of their removal, we obtain a standard tableau
\theta
and the intervals of this longest chain are actually enumerated by the Schur function
s_{n}
. In general, the triangular tableau does not correspond to a longest chain in the
\nu
-Tamari lattice. See for example the triangular tableau of
(4,2,1)
given on Figure
21
and the
\nu
-Tamari lattice shown on Figure
20
. After removing the cells labeled by
7
and
6
, removing the cell labeled by
5
forces us to remove the cell
4
at the same time.

This is why we introduce the notion of top-down tableau.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition of
n
. The top-down tableau of
\lambda
is given by labeling with
n
the last cell of the top line, then with
n-1
the last cell of the line below and so on until we have labeled exactly one cell per line. We then redo the process on the remaining cells.

Figure
23
shows an example of this construction on the partition
(4,2,1)
.

\begin{array}[t]{*{4}c}\cline{1-1}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$7$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\

.
Construction of the top-down tableau for
\lambda=(4,2,1)
.

Proposition 5.6
.

The top-down tableau corresponds to a maximal chain in the
\nu
-Tamari lattice. In other words, it is possible to remove the cells one by one in the reverse order of the labels by following covering relations of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice.

Proof.

Let
\lambda
be a triangular partition of
n
,
k
such that
1\leq k\leq n
, and let
\mu
be the subpartition formed by the cells with labels
c\leq k
. Let
\tilde{\mu}
be the subpartition formed by the cells with labels
c<k
. We need to prove that
\tilde{\mu}
can be obtained through a rotation on
\mu
. By definition of
\mu
and of a standard tableau, the cell labeled by
k
is at a corner and at the end of a certain line
i
of
\mu
, so the rotation of
\mu
at line
i
is well defined and will remove at least this cell. The rotation also removes a cell from line
i-1
(if it exists) if
\lambda_{i-1}-\mu_{i-1}>\lambda_{i}-\mu_{i}
. This is not possible by the top-down construction as every time a cell on line
i-1
has been removed, a cell on line
i
has been removed just before.
∎

Using the top-down tableau instead of the triangular tableau, for the partition
(4,2,1)
we find that the
\nu
-Tamari lattice is a solution to Problem
2
. Indeed, we get that (
32
) is symmetric and equal to the expansion of

(34)

s_{2,1,1}+s_{3,2}+s_{4,1}+s_{5,1}+s_{7}

in
q,t,1
(see
[
Pon25
]
for the computation and test).

What is true for
(4,2,1)
is true for many other partitions but not for all. Indeed, sometimes it cannot be true because the top-down tableau is not sim-sym. This is the case in particular for
(4,3,1)
, you can check that none of the sim-sym tableaux presented in Figure
22
are the top-down tableau which is given on Figure
24
. We see an example of triangular Dyck path with area equals to
3
and sim equals to
2
. It can be checked that there is no triangular Dyck path with area equals to
2
and sim equals to
3
.

1
2
4
6
3
5
7
8

\area_{\lambda}(\mu)=3
,
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=2

Figure 24
.
Counter example of area, sim symmetry on the top-down tableau of
\lambda=(4,3,1)
.

Our exploration leads us to emit the following conjecture.

Conjecture 3
.

If
\lambda
is a triangular partition such that its top-down tableau
\theta
is a sim-sym tableau, there exists a Schur-positive symmetric function
\lambda
such that

(35)

A_{\lambda}(q,t,1)=\sum_{\tau\preceq\mu}q^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\tau,\mu)}

where the sum runs over intervals in the
\nu
-Tamari lattice of
\lambda
.

We have checked with computations:

•

Up to
n=21
, for all partition
\lambda
such that the top-down tableau of
\lambda
is sim-sym, the polynomial (
32
) is also symmetric. The biggest non trivial partition tested is
\lambda=(8,6,4,2,1)
where the
\nu
-Tamari lattice has
416
elements and
17388
intervals.

•

For the same partitions, up to size
21
, we have checked that the polynomial can be obtained at the expansion of a Schur positive function in 2 variables (as we are missing the last variable
r
, we cannot get directly the expression as a Schur function in three variables, but the Schur positivity in
2
variables is a necessary condition).

•

François Bergeron provided us with a list of potential
A_{\lambda}
Schur functions and we checked that the polynomial (
32
) was indeed the expansion of these functions in
q,t,1
. We have provided the list of functions on Table
1
.

For the last point, note that these
A_{\lambda}
functions are related to diagonal harmonic polynomials and are actually very difficult to compute. For some of them, the expression computed by François Bergeon is only conjectural and might be missing some terms. Actually, when first tested against our conjecture, we noted that some terms were
not
equal to our enumeration when expanded on
q,t,1
. It was the case of
A_{6,4,2,1}
and
A_{7,5,3,1}
. Looking more closely, we noticed that the expansion would be equal if some extra Schur terms were added to the formula that had been provided to us: we added
s_{3,2,2}
to
A_{6,4,2,1}
, and
s_{6,2,2}+s_{5,2,2}+s_{4,2,2}
to
A_{7,5,3,1}
. Discussing with François Bergeron, we concluded that those terms were difficult to get through his computation method and conjectured that the actual
A_{\lambda}
value contained them.

All the code for these tests is available on
[
Pon25
]
. Note that we have only included in Table
1
partitions of length greater than or equal to
3
. Indeed, for partitions of length
1
, the result is trivial, for partitions of length
2
, we have an exact formula and can prove the conjecture. First notice that the top-down tableau of a
2
-partition is always sim-sym.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a
2
-partition, then the top-down tableau of
\lambda
is the maximal row-regular tableau of
\lambda
.
The labels on the upper line are given by
m-n+2,m-n+4,\dots,m+n
. The labels on the lower line are given by
1,2,\dots,m-n,m-n+1,m-n+3,\dots,m+n-1
.

In particular, the top-down tableau of
\lambda
is sim-sym.

Proof.

The top-down tableau labels the cells from both line alternatively starting from the upper line until the upper line is filled. This is the definition of the maximal row-regular tableau given in Definition
4.5
. The fact that it is sim-sym is a consequence of Theorem
4.10
.
∎

We prove the following theorem in Section
5.4
which implies in particular that Conjecture
3
is true on
2
-partitions.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
a triangular partition,
\theta
its top-down tableau and

(36)

A_{\lambda}:=\sum_{0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)}s_{m+n-2d,d}.

Then

(37)

A_{\lambda}(q,t,1)=\sum_{\tau\preceq\mu}q^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\tau,\mu)}

summed over the intervals of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice for
\lambda
.

Note that the formula is the same as in Theorem
4.12
but now expanded on three variables instead of two (with the tjirs variable equals to
1
). We show an example on Figure
25
.

\displaystyle A_{3,1}

\displaystyle=s_{4}+s_{2,1}

\displaystyle A_{3,1}(q,t,1)

\displaystyle=q^{4}+q^{3}t+q^{2}t^{2}+qt^{3}+t^{4}+q^{3}+2q^{2}t+2qt^{2}

\displaystyle+t^{3}+2q^{2}+3qt+2t^{2}+2q+2t+1

\displaystyle A_{3,1}(1,1,1)

\displaystyle=23

Figure 25
.
Interval enumeration for
\lambda=(3,1)
.

5.3.
Structure of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice on
2
-partitions

To prove Theorem
5.8
, we need to understand better the specific structure of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice on
2
-partitions. The geometric realizations of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice has been studied in
[
CPS16
]
. From their work, we know that in the case of
2
-partitions, the lattice forms a subdivision of a pentagon. In particular, it is a polygonal lattice, its Hasse diagram is planar and made of “glued”
2
d-faces as we see on the example of Figure
26
. The faces are either pentagons or squares.

0
