Figure 26
.
The structure of the
\lambda
-lattice on a
2
-partition, with
\lambda=(7,3)
. Elements share the same
\theta
-
sim
between two green dashed lines.

Let us explicit the definitions and properties that are needed for this paper. For the rest of this section,
\lambda=(m,n)
is always a triangular partition and
\theta
is its top down tableau.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
a subpartition of
\lambda
.

If
m-i>n-j
, then a
\nu
-Tamari rotation can be applied on the lower line of
\mu
. We call it a
lower-rotation
on
\mu
and write it
\underline{r}(\mu)
. We have that
\underline{r}(\mu)=(m-i-1,n-j)
.

If
n-j>0
, then a
\nu
-Tamari rotation can be applied on the upper line of
\mu
. We call it an
upper-rotation
on
\mu
and write it
\overline{r}(\mu)
. We have that

•

if
i\leq j
, then
\overline{r}(\mu)=(m-i,n-j-1)
(only one cell is removed);

•

otherwise,
\overline{r}(\mu)=(m-i-1,n-j-1)
(two cells are removed).

See examples on Figure
26
where
\lambda=(7,3)
. The upper-rotations are all cover relations going in direction north-west in the picture, for example between
(7,3)
and
(7,2)
. You can see that they remove only one cell of the partition
\mu
. The other cover relations (going north, north-east, and east) are lower-rotations. The ones going east or north-east remove only one cell. For example, between
(7,3)
and
(6,3)
, we have
m-m^{\prime}=7-7=0\leq 0=3-3=n-n^{\prime}
, between
(7,2)
and
(6,2)
, we have
m-m^{\prime}=7-7=0\leq 1=3-2=n-n^{\prime}
. The cover relations going north are upper rotations which remove two cells. For example, from
(6,3)
to
(5,2)
, we have
m-m^{\prime}=7-6=1>0=3-3=n-n^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This is a direct application of the definition of
\nu
-Tamari rotations on
2
-partitions.
∎

Definition 5.10
.

Let
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
be a subpartition of
\lambda
. If one can apply both an upper and a lower rotation on
\mu
, we call the interval between
\mu
and
\overline{r}(\mu)\wedge\underline{r}(\mu)
a
polygon
.

These so-called polygons are actual 2-dimensional faces in the geometric realization of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice. They also are polygons in the sense of a
polygonal lattice
,
i.e.
, the interval is formed by two chains which meet only at their ends. These are known properties of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice but as it is very simple in our case, we give a direct proof and a characterization of the different types of polygons that can appear.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
be a subpartition of
\lambda
such that
m-i>n-j>0
(both upper and lower rotations can be applied at
\mu
).

If
i=j
, then we call the polygon starting at
\mu
is a
pentagon
(as it contains
5
elements), it is formed by the union of two chains:
(m-i,n-j)\prec(m-i,n-j-1)\prec(m-i-1,n-j-1)\prec(m-i-2,n-j-1)
and
(m-i,n-j)\prec(m-i-1,n-j)\prec(m-i-2,n-j-1)
.

If
i<j
, then we call the polygon starting at
\mu
a
small square
(as it contains
4
elements), it is formed by the union of two chains:
(m-i,n-j)\prec(m-i,n-j-1)\prec(m-i-1,n-j-1)
and
(m-i,n-j)\prec(m-i-1,n-j)\prec(m-i-1,n-j-1)
.

If
i>j
, then we call the polygon starting at
\mu
a
big square
(as it contains
4
elements), it is formed by the union of two chains:
(m-i,n-j)\prec(m-i-1,n-j-1)\prec(m-i-2,n-j-1)
and
(m-i,n-j)\prec(m-i-1,n-j)\prec(m-i-2,n-j-1)
.

Proof.

(m-i,n-i)
(m-i,n-i-1)
(m-i-1,n-i-1)
(m-i-2,n-i-1)
(m-i-1,n-i)
\overline{r}
\underline{r}
\underline{r}
\underline{r}
\overline{r}
m-i>n-i>0
n-i>0
i+1>i
m-i>n-i-1
m-i-1>n-i-1
(m-i-2,n-i)
(m-i-3,n-i-1)
\underline{r}
\overline{r}
\underline{r}
i+2>i

Figure 27
.
Proof of the pentagon case.

(m-i,n-j)
(m-i,n-j-1)
(m-i-1,n-j-1)
(m-i-1,n-j)
\overline{r}
\underline{r}
\underline{r}
\overline{r}
m-i>n-j>0
i<j
n-j>0
i+1\leq j
m-i>n-j-1

Figure 28
.
Proof of the small square case.

(m-i,n-j)
(m-i-1,n-j-1)
(m-i-1,n-j)
(m-i-2,n-j-1)
\overline{r}
\underline{r}
\underline{r}
\overline{r}
m-i>n-j>0
i>j
n-j>0
i+1>j
m-i-1>n-j-1

Figure 29
.
Proof of the big square case.

The proof for the three different cases are given in Figures
27
(pentagon),
28
(small square), and
29
(big square). First note that as
m-i>n-j>0
, we have
m-i\geq 2
and
n-j\geq 1
so all subpartitions in the polygons are well defined. Following the graph, you can see that elements of the chains are obtained through proper
\nu
-Tamari rotations as the equalities and inequalities in red are satisfied. In the small and big square cases, the lower and upper rotations commute so it is clear that the top element is indeed the join of
\overline{r}(\mu)
and
\underline{r}(\mu)
and that no other rotation can be applied while staying in the interval. In the pentagon case, we use Lemma
5.2
to complete the proof. We have written in dashed blue the lower rotation between
(m-i-1,n-i)
and
(m-i-2,n-j)
(only possible if
m-i-1>n-i
). Following Lemma
5.2
,
(m-i-2,n-i)
is the only subpartition, beside the five subpartitions of the pentagon, which might belong to the interval between
(m-i,n-i)
and
(m-i-2,n-i-1)
. We see in the figure that it is not the case as we obtain
(m-i-3,n-i-1)\succ(m-i-2,n-i-1)
by applying an upper rotation.
∎

Definition 5.12
.

Let
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
be a subpartition of
\lambda
, we say that
\mu
is

•

on the
left side
if
i<j
;

•

in the
center
if
i=j
;

•

on the
right side
if
i>j
.

In the example of Figure
26
, we have
6
subparitions on the left side (
(7,2)
,
(7,1)
,
(7)
,
(6,1)
,
(6)
, and
(5)
),
4
subpartitions in the center (
(7,3)
,
(6,2)
,
(5,1)
, and
(4)
) and the rest on the right side. In general, the small squares are all on the left side, while the big squares are all on the right side. We have
n+1
elements
(m-i,n-i)
in the center (
i=0,1,\dots,n
). If
i<n
, then they correspond to the minimal element of a pentagon, so there are exactly
n
pentagons.

Now that we understand the structure, we can easily compute the distance between two elements.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
be a subpartition of
\lambda
such that
\tau
is on the right side and let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
with
\mu\succ\tau
. Then
\mu
is also on the right side and
\dist(\tau,\mu)=\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}
.

For example, the distance between
(5,2)
and
(2,1)
in the lattice of Figure
26
is
5-2=3
.

Proof.

Let
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
be a subpartition of
\lambda
which is on the right side. Then
\tau_{1}=m-i
and
\tau_{2}=n-j
with
i>j
. By Proposition
5.9
, by applying an upper rotation, we obtain
(m-i-1,n-j-1)
which is still on the right side. Similarly, by applying a lower rotation, we obtain
(m-i-1,n-j)
which is also still on the right side. So any element covering
\tau
stays on the right side and by induction also any subpartition
\mu\succ\tau
. Besides, the length of the lower part is reduced by one by any of the two rotations which means that any chain between
\tau
and
\mu
is of length
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}
.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
be tw subpartitions of
\lambda
such that
\tau
is on the right side. Then
\tau\preceq\mu
if and only if
\mu\subseteq\tau
and
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}\geq\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
.

Proof.

Suppose that we have
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
on the right side and
\tau
such that
\mu\subseteq\tau
and
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}\geq\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
. We prove that
\tau\preceq\mu
by induction on
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}
. Suppose first that
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}=0
, then we can reach
\mu
from
\tau
by applying a series of
\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
lower-rotations which each decrease the lower part by one (and do not change the upper part) and
\tau\preceq\mu
. Now suppose that
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}>0
, we apply an upper rotation and as
\tau
is on the right side, we obtain
\tau^{\prime}=(\tau_{1}-1,\tau_{2}-1)
and by Proposition
5.13
,
\tau^{\prime}
is still on the right side. Besides
(\tau_{1}-1)-\mu_{1}=(\tau_{1}-\mu_{1})-1\geq(\tau_{2}-\mu_{2})-1=(\tau_{2}-1)-\mu_{2}
and we obtain the result by induction.

Now suppose that
\tau\preceq\mu
. This implies in particular that
\mu\subseteq\tau
. This also means that there is a chain between
\tau
and
\mu
where a
\nu
-Tamari rotation is applied at each step. This chain contains exactly
\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
upper rotations, because only upper rotations can decrease the upper row. As
\tau
is on the right side, it is also the case of any subpartition in the chain (from Proposition
5.13
), so all the upper rotations also decrease the lower row by one. This implies that
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}\geq\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
be two subpartitions such that
\tau
is in the center or on the left side and
\mu\subseteq\tau
,
i.e.
,
\tau_{1}\geq\mu_{1}
and
\tau_{2}\geq\mu_{2}
. Then

(38)

(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\prec(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}-1)\prec\dots\prec(\tau_{1},\mu_{2})\prec(\tau_{1}-1,\mu_{2})\prec\dots\prec(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

is a well defined chain of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice.

For example, on Figure
26
, take
\tau=(7,2)
and
\mu=(3,1)
, then the chain is
(7,2)\prec(7,1)\prec(6,1)\prec(5,1)\prec(4,1)\prec(3,1)
.

Proof.

Let
\tau^{\prime}=(\tau_{1},\mu_{2})
and prove that, when
\tau^{\prime}\neq\mu
, the chain between
\tau^{\prime}
and
\mu
is well defined. This is immediate: at each step, we remove one cell from the lower line (which is always possible because
\tau_{1}>\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}
) which corresponds to a lower rotation.
Now we prove that the chain between
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
is well defined by induction on
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}
. If
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}=0
, it is the empty chain and there is nothing to prove. Now we write
\tau=(m-i,n-j)
and
\tau^{\prime}=(m-i,n-j^{\prime})
with
j<j^{\prime}
. In particular,
n-j>0
and we can apply an upper rotation on
\tau
. As
\tau
is in the center or on the left side, we have by hypothesis that
i\leq j
, so
\overline{r}(\tau)=(m-i,n-j-1)
which is the next element in the chain. Beside, we have
i<j+1
, so
(m-i,n-j-1)
is on the left side and we can conclude the proof by induction.
∎

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
be two subpartitions of
\lambda
such that
\tau
is in the center or on the left side. Then
\tau\preceq\mu
if and only if
\mu\subseteq\tau
and in this case
\dist(\tau,\mu)=\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}+\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
.

Proof.

Note that we know from Lemma
5.2
that
\mu\subseteq\tau
is a necessary condition to have
\tau\preceq\mu
. The construction of Lemma
5.15
proves that in the case where
\tau
is in the center or on the left side, it is also sufficient.

Let
a=\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}+\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
which is also equals to
\area(\mu)-\area(\tau)
. Any rotation applied on
\tau
removes at least one cell from
\tau
(thus increasing the area). So any chain from
\tau
to
\mu
has a length smaller than or equal to
a
. We get
\dist(\tau,\mu)\leq a
. As the distance is defined as the length of the longest chain between
\tau
and
\mu
, we only have to exhibit a chain of length
a
to prove the result. We use the chain of Lemma
5.15
.
∎

We now use the
sim
to partition the lattice into subsets.

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
s
be an integer value such that
0\leq s\leq m+n
, we write
E(s)
the set of subpartitions
\mu
of
\lambda
such that
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=s
. We have the following.

(1)

If
s\leq m-n
, then
E(s)=\{(s),(s,1),(s,2),\dots(s,\min(s,n))\}
. In particular
|E(s)|=\min(s,n)+1
.
All subpartitions of
E(s)
are all on the right side except for the subpartition
(m-n)\in E(m-n)
which is in the center.

(2)

If
s>m-n
and is of same parity as
m+n
, then there exists an integer
i
with
0\leq i<n
such that
s=m+n-2i
and we have
E(s)=\{(m-i,n-j);0\leq j\leq i\}
. In particular,
|E(s)|=i+1
. For all
s
, the subpartition
(m-i,n-i)
is in the center and the other subpartitions of
E(s)
are on the right side.

(3)

If
s>m-n
and of different parity than
m+n
, then there there exists an integer
j
with
1\leq j\leq n
such that
s=m+n-2j+1
and we have
E(s)=\{(m-i,n-j);0\leq i<j\}
. In particular,
|E(s)|=j
. For all
s
, all subpartitions of
E(s)
are on the left side.

This corresponds to the partition of the lattice by the green lines on Figure
26
. We show specific examples from this lattice on Figure
30
. You can see for example that for
s=3<7-3
, we have
4
subpartitions
\mu
with
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=3
which are
(3)
,
(3,1)
,
(3,2)
, and
(3,3)
(all on the left side). For
s=6
, same parity than
10
,
s=10-2\times 2
and there are
3
subpartitions
\mu
with
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=6
:
(5,1)
,
(5,2)
, and
(5,3)
(with
(5,1)
in the center and the other on the right side). For
s=7
, different parity than
10
, we have
7=10-2\times 2+1
, we get
2
subpartitions
\mu
with
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=7
:
(6,1)
, and
(7,1)
(both on the left side).

) with

.

Example of Case (
3
) with
s=7
.

Figure 30
.
Examples of subpartitions with same sim values.

Proof.

For the sake of the proof, we define
E(s)
constructively using the descriptions given in the different cases and first prove that for all
\mu\in E(s)
,
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=s
.

Proof for Case (
1
).

Let
s\leq m-n
and
E(s):=\{(s,k);0\leq k\leq\min(s,n)\}
.
Let
\mu=(s,k)
with
k\leq\min(s,n)
. As
k\leq s
,
\mu
is a well defined partition. And because
k\leq n
, it is indeed a subpartition of
\lambda
. Beside, as
s=m-i
with
i\geq n
,
\mu
is necessary on the right side except when
i=n
and
k=0
, then it is on the center. We also set
\tau:=(s,0)
. In particular,
\tau\subseteq\mu
.

As the top-down tableau of
\lambda
is the maximal row-regular tableau of
\lambda
described in Proposition
5.7
, the first
m-n+1
cells of the lower row are labeled
1,2,\dots,m-n+1
. As
s\leq m-n
, this means that the cells of
\tau
have labels
1,2\dots,s
. In particular,
\tau
has no deficit and
\ssim_{\theta}(\tau)=s
. Now if there is a cell in
\mu
and not in
\tau
, it is on the upper row with label
m-n+2i
and
0\leq i\leq\min(s,n)
. The cell
m-n+2i+1
is on the lower row and not in
\mu
. In other words, whenever we add a cell on the upper row of
\tau
, we add a sim cell (the new cell) but create a deficit in the cell below, thus keeping the sim value constant. See an example on Figure
30(a)
. We find
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=\ssim_{\theta}(\tau)=s
.

Proof for Case (
2
).

Let
s>m-n
and with same parity than
m+n
. Then there exists a certain integer
i\geq 0
such that
s=m+n-2i
. As we have
s>m-n
, we obtain
i<n
. Let
E(s):=\{(m-i,n-j);0\leq j\leq i\}
and
\mu=(m-i,n-j)\in E(s)
. Note that
\mu
is well defined as the conditions on
i
and on
2
-triangular partition implies that
m-i\geq n
. Beside
\mu
is on the right side by definition except when
j=i
, then it is in the center.
We also set
\tau:=(m-i,n-i)
. In particular, we have
\tau\subseteq\mu
.

The labels of the top-down tableau given in Proposition
5.7
can be written as:
1,2,\dots,m-n
followed by
m+n-2(n-1)-1,m+n-2(n-2)-1,\dots,m+n-1
on the lower row and
m+n-2(n-1),m+n-2(n-2),\dots,m+n
on the upper row. In particular, the labels of the cells in
\tau
are
1,2\dots m-n
followed by
m+n-2(n-1)-1,\dots,m-2i-1
on the lower row and
m+n-2(n-1),\dots,m+n-2i
on the upper row. This corresponds to all labels
k
for
k\leq m+n-2i
. See for example Figure
30(b)
where
m=7
,
n=3
, and
i=2
. The subpartition
\tau
is
(5,1)
and you can see that it corresponds to all cells with labels smaller than or equal to
6
. It means in particular that there are no deficit in
\tau
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\tau)=m-i+n-i=s
. Now, if there is a cell in
\mu
but not in
\tau
, then it is on the upper row with a label
m+n-2k
with
k<i
, the cell with label
m+n-2k-1
is on the lower row and not in
\mu
: it creates a deficit. In other words, whenever we add a cell on the upper row of
\tau
, we add sim cell (the new cell) but create a deficit in the cell below, thus keeping the sim value constant. We find that
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=\ssim_{\theta}(\tau)=s
.

Proof for Case (
3
).

Let
s>m-n
and with different parity than
m+n
. Then there exists a certain integer
j\geq 1
such that
s=m+n-2j+1
. As we have
s>m-n
, we find that
j\leq n
. Let
E(s):=\{(m-i,n-j);0\leq i<j\}
and
\mu=(m-i,n-j)\in E(s)
. As
m\geq n
and
i<j
, we have
m-i>m-j
and
\mu
is a well defined partition. It is on the left side by definition. We also set
\tau:=(m-j+1,n-j)
. In particular, we have
\tau\subseteq\mu
.

Following the above description of the top-down tableau, the cells of
\tau
are labeled by
1,2,\dots,m-n
then
m+n-2(n-1)-1,\dots,m-2(j-1)-1
on the lower row, and
m+n-2(n-1),\dots,m+n-2j
on the upper row. This corresponds to all labels
k
for
k\leq m-2(j-1)-1
. See for example Figure
30(c)
where
m=7
,
n=3
, and
j=1
. The subpartition
\tau
is
(6,1)
and you can see that it corresponds to all cells with labels smaller than or equal to
7
. It means in particular that there are no deficit in
\tau
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\tau)=m-j+1+n-j=s
. Now, if there is a cell in
\mu
but not in
\tau
, then it is on the lower row with a label
m+n-2k-1
with
k<j-1
, the cell with label
m+n-2(k+1)
is on the upper row and not in
\mu
: it creates a deficit. In other words, whenever we add a cell on the lower row of
\tau
, we add sim cell (the new cell) but create a deficit in the cell below
m+n-2(k+1)
, thus keeping the sim value constant. We find that
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=\ssim_{\theta}(\tau)=s
.

We have proven that for each
s
,
E(s)\subseteq\{\mu;\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=s\}
. To prove the equality, we show that any
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
belongs to exactly one
E(s)
as defined above. Suppose first that
\mu
is on the left side,
i.e.

i<j
and take
s=m+n-2j+1
. As
j\leq n
, we have that
s>m-n
. It is also of different parity than
m+n
and
\mu\in E(s)
(Case (
3
)) by definition. Now suppose that
j\leq i<n
and take
s=m+n-2i
. We have that
s>m-n
and of same parity than
m+n
: by definition,
\mu\in E(s)
(Case (
2
)). Finally, suppose that
i\geq n
and take
s=m-i\leq m-n
. As
\mu
is a partition,
n-j\leq m-i
and also
n-j\leq n
by definition so
\mu=(s,k)
with
0\leq k\leq\min(s,n)
and
\mu\in E(s)
(Case
1
).
∎

Corollary 5.18
.

Let
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
be a subpartition of
\lambda
. Then

•

if
\mu
is in the center or on the right side, then
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m-i+\min(0,n-i)
(the value depends only of
i
);

•

if
\mu
is on the left side, then
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m+n-2j+1
(the value depends only of
j
).

Proof.

Suppose first that
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
is in the center or on the right side. In particular
j\leq i
and we are in Case (
1
) or Case (
2
) of Proposition
5.17
. Suppose that
n-i>0
and take
s=m+n-2i
. We have
s>m-n
and
\mu\in E(s)
following Case (
2
). On the other hand, if
n-i\leq 0
, we have
\mu\in E(m-i)
following Case (
1
) as
m-i\leq m-n
and
n-j\leq\min(m-i,n)
.

Now if
\mu
is on the left side, we are necessarily in Case
3
and
\mu\in E(m+n-2j+1)
.
∎

5.4.
Proof of the conjecture for
2
-partitions

For the time being, we write

(39)

P_{m,n}(q,t):=\sum_{\tau\preceq\mu}q^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}t^{\ssim_{\theta}(\tau,\mu)}

summed over the intervals of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice for
\lambda=(m,n)
. Our goal is to prove Theorem
5.8
,
i.e.
that
P_{m,n}(q,t)=A_{\lambda}(q,t,1)
. We proceed by induction. More precisely, we are proving that for
n>0
,

(40)

P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}=A_{m,n}-A_{m-1,n-1}.

In other words, both quantities grow the same way. Regrettably, our proof does not give a bijection between the intervals and the monomials of the Schur function. This shows that even in the “simple” case of
2
-partitions, the question of finding a bijective proof is difficult.

We start with the base case of the induction.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
m\geq 0
, then

(41)

P_{m,0}(q,t)=A_{m,0}(q,t,1)=\sum_{s=0}^{m}q^{s}\left(\sum_{d=0}^{m-s}t^{d}\right)

Proof.

Remember that
A_{m,0}=s_{m}
. Now following the definition of Schur functions, we have

(42)

s_{m}(q,t,r)=\sum_{T\in\mathrm{SSYT}(m)}q^{T_{1}}t^{T_{2}}r^{T_{3}}

summed over the semi-standard Young tableaux of shape
m
and values in
\{1,2,3\}
, where
T_{i}
is the number of occurrences of
i
in
T
. The tableau
T
is made of single horizontal line. As it is semi-standard, the line is filled by a certain number of
1
, followed by a certain number of
2
and the rest filled with
3
. Let
s
be the number of
1
: it varies between
0
and
m
. For each
s
, you can have between
0
and
m-s
values
2
in the tableau. This gives the expected formula.

Now the
\nu
-Tamari lattice for
\lambda=m
is just the total order
m\prec m-1\prec\dots\prec 0
. If
\mu
is subpartition of
\lambda
, then
\mu=m-i
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m-i
. Any subpartition
\tau\preceq\mu
is of the form
m-j
with
j\leq i
, the distance between
\tau
and
\mu
is
i-j
. We find that

(43)

P_{m,0}(q,t)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}q^{m-i}\sum_{j=0}^{i}t^{i-j}

which gives the expected formula by a simple change of variable.
∎

Now, for
n>0
, our goal is to compute
P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}
. First notice that if
m,n
is a triangular partition and
n>0
, then
m-1,n-1
is also a triangular partition. Besides the
\nu
-Tamari lattice for
\lambda=(m,n)
“contains” the
\nu
-Tamari lattice for
m-1,n-1
. The
(m,n)
lattice can be obtained by “adding a line” under the
m-1,n-1
lattice as illustrated on Figure
31
. We formalize this result in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.20
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
a triangular partition such that
n>0
, and
\tau\preceq\mu
an interval in the
\nu
-Tamari lattice of
\lambda
with
\tau=(m-i,n-j)
and
i,j\geq 1
. Then
\tau
and
\mu
are subpartitions of
(m-1,n-1)
, they form an interval in the
\nu
-Tamari lattice of
(m-1,n-1)
and the
sim
and
distance
of the interval is the same in both lattices.

3,3

Figure 31
.
The recursive construction of
\nu
-Tamari lattices for
2
-partitions

Proof.

Let us call
L_{1}
the
\nu
-Tamari lattice of
\lambda=(m,n)
and
L_{0}
the
\nu
-Tamari lattice of
(m-1,n-1)
.
We have
\tau=(m-i,n-j)
and by hypothesis
i\geq 1
and
j\geq 1
. This means that
\tau
is a subpartition of
(m-1,n-1)
. As
\mu
is included in
\tau
, this is also the case for
\mu
. So
(\tau,\mu)
is an interval of
L_{0}
. Now suppose that
i<j
(
i.e.
,
\mu
is on the left side in
L_{1}
), this implies that
i-1<j-1
and
\tau
is also on the left side in
L_{0}
. The same reasoning also applies for the center and the right side and also for
\mu
: whatever sides
\tau
and
\mu
are in
L_{1}
, they are still on these same sides in
L_{0}
. In particular, by applying Propositions
5.16
and
5.13
the distance between
\tau
and
\mu
is the same in both
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
.

Now, we write
\mu=(m-i^{\prime},n-j^{\prime})=(m-1-(i^{\prime}-1),n-1-(j^{\prime}-1))
. Suppose first that
\mu
is on the left side. By applying Corollary
5.18
, we obtain that
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m+n-2j^{\prime}+1
in
L_{1}
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m-1+n-1-2(j^{\prime}-1)+1
in
L_{0}
: these two values are equal. If
\mu
is in the center or on the right side, either
n-i^{\prime}>0
and so is
(n-1)-(i^{\prime}-1)
. In this case, we have
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m+n-2i^{\prime}
in
L_{1}
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=(m-1)+(n-1)-2(i^{\prime}-1)
in
L_{0}
. Or
n-i^{\prime}\leq 0
and we have
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m-i^{\prime}
in
L_{1}
and
\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)=m-1-(i^{\prime}-1)
in
L_{0}
. In all cases, the
sim
of
\mu
is the same relatively to
(m,n)
and
(m-1,n-1)
.
∎

This proposition implies that the difference between
P_{m,n}
and
P_{m-1,n-1}
can be computed by enumerating intervals
(\tau,\mu)
where at least one of the subpartitions is not a subparition of
(m-1,n-1)
. As
\mu
is included in
\tau
, these are all intervals where
\tau=(m-i,n)
or
\tau=(m,n-j)
,
i.e.
,
\tau
is on the “red line” of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice as shown on Figure
31
. This tells us that for all
\mu
of the lattice, we need to enumerate the intervals
(\tau,\mu)
with
\tau
on the red line depending on the distance between
\tau
and
\mu
. We define the following polynomial depending on a subpartition
\mu
.

Definition 5.21
.

Let
\mu
be a subpartition of
\lambda
, then

(44)

f_{\mu}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau=(m-i^{\prime},n-j^{\prime})\preceq\mu\\
i^{\prime}=0\text{ or }j^{\prime}=0\end{subarray}}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
be a subpartition of
\lambda=(m,n)
,

•

if
\mu
is on the left side, then

(45)

f_{\mu}(t)=\sum_{d=i}^{i+j}t^{d};

•

if
\mu
is on the right side, then

(46)

f_{\mu}(t)=\sum_{d=j}^{i+j}t^{d};

•

and if
\mu
is in the center

(47)

f_{\mu}(t)=\sum_{d=i}^{i+j}t^{d}=\sum_{d=j}^{i+j}t^{d}.

Proof.

We split the sum into two parts,

(48)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau=(m-i^{\prime},n-j^{\prime})\preceq\mu\\
i^{\prime}=0\text{ or }j^{\prime}=0\end{subarray}}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau=(m-i^{\prime},n)\preceq\mu\\
i^{\prime}>0\end{subarray}}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}+\sum_{\tau=(m,n-j^{\prime})\preceq\mu}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}.

Let us look first at the second part of the sum,
i.e.
, the subpartitions
\tau\preceq\mu
where
\tau=(m,n-j^{\prime})
. In particular,
\tau
is on the left side or in the center. Then by Proposition
5.16
, we have
\tau\preceq\mu
if and only if
\mu\subseteq\tau
,
i.e.
,
j^{\prime}\leq j
. Besides, we have
\dist(\tau,\mu)=i+j-j^{\prime}
. We get

(49)

\sum_{\tau=(m,n-j^{\prime})\preceq\mu}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}=\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}t^{i+j-j^{\prime}}=\sum_{d=i}^{i+j}t^{d}.

We now look at the first part of sum,
i.e.
, the subpartitions
\tau\preceq\mu
where
\tau=(m-i^{\prime},n)
with
i^{\prime}>0
. In particular,
\tau
is on the right side. By Proposition
5.13
, this implies that
\mu
also is on the right side. In other words, if
\mu
is on the left side or in the center, this sum is equal to
0
as there is no such subpartition
\tau
. We thus obtain (
45
) and also (
47
) because if
\mu
is in the center then
i=j
which gives the second part of the equality.

If
\mu
is on the right side, then by Proposition
5.14
, we have
\tau\leq\mu
if and only if
i^{\prime}\leq i
(as
\mu\subseteq\tau
), and
j\leq i-i^{\prime}
(as
\tau_{1}-\mu_{1}\geq\tau_{2}-\mu_{2}
). This gives that
1\leq i^{\prime}\leq i-j
. Besides, by Proposition
5.13
, we have
\dist(\tau,\mu)=i-i^{\prime}
and we get

(50)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau=(m-i^{\prime},n)\preceq\mu\\
i^{\prime}>0\end{subarray}}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}=\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-j}t^{i-i^{\prime}}=\sum_{d=j}^{i-1}t^{d}.

By summing (
49
) and (
50
), we get the expected formula (
46
)
∎

You can check this result on different examples on Figure
31
. See the subpatition
(6,1)
of
(7,3)
which is on the left side with
i=1
and
j=2
. All supartitions
\tau\preceq\mu
are on the left side or in the center. Three of them are on the red line:
(7,1)
at distance
1
,
(7,2)
at distance
2
, and
(7,3)
at distance
3
. We get indeed
t+t^{2}+t^{3}
which is the sum of
t^{d}
for
d=i=1
up to
d=i+j=3
.

Now take
\mu=(2,1)
which is on the right side with
i=5
and
j=2
. We look at the subpartions
\tau\preceq\mu
on the red line. On the right side, we have
(4,3)
,
(5,3)
, and
(6,3)
with respective distances
2
,
3
, and
4
. Then (
50
) gives
t^{2}+t^{3}+t^{4}
. On the left side or in the center, we have
(7,1)
,
(7,2)
and
(7,3)
with respective distances
5
,
6
, and
7
. Then (
49
) gives
t^{5}+t^{6}+t^{7}
. By summing both, we get (
46
)
t^{2}+\dots+t^{7}
which is indeed the sum of
t^{d}
for
d
between
j=2
and
i+j=7
.

Proposition 5.23
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition with
n>0
, then

(51)

\displaystyle P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{m-n}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\min(s,n)}\sum_{d=n-k}^{m+n-k-s}t^{d}\right)q^{s}

(52)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{d=j}^{i+j}t^{d}\right)q^{m+n-2i}

(53)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\sum_{d=i}^{i+j}t^{d}\right)q^{m+n-2j+1}

Proof.

Let
(\tau,\mu)
be an interval of the
\nu
-Tamari lattice for
\lambda=(m,n)
. If
\tau
is a subpartition of
(m-1,n-1)
, so is
\mu
and the monomial
q^{\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)}
of
P_{m,n}
is canceled by the same monomial appearing in
P_{m-1,n-1}
by Proposition
5.20
. We obtain

(54)

\displaystyle P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu\subseteq\lambda}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\preceq\mu\\
\tau=(m-i^{\prime},n-j^{\prime})\\
i^{\prime}=0\text{ or }j^{\prime}=0\end{subarray}}q^{\ssim_{\theta}(\mu)}t^{\dist(\tau,\mu)},

(55)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{m+n}\left(\sum_{\mu\in E(s)}f_{\mu}(t)\right)q^{s}.

Now we split this sum into three parts depending on the value of
s
following the three cases of Proposition
5.17
.

(56)

\displaystyle P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{m-n}\left(\sum_{\mu\in E(s)}f_{\mu}(t)\right)q^{s}

(57)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(\sum_{\mu\in E(m+n-2i)}f_{\mu}(t)\right)q^{m+n-2i}

(58)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\mu\in E(m+n-2j+1)}f_{\mu}(t)\right)q^{m+n-2j+1}.

Now we need to prove that these three sums given on (
56
), (
57
), and (
58
) correspond respectively to the sums given on (
51
), (
52
), and (
53
).

Case
1
.

Let us take
s\leq m-n
, then
\mu\in E(s)
gives that
\mu=(s,k)
with
0\leq k\leq\min(s,n)
. Let us set
i:=m-s
and
j:=n-k
so that
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
. We have that
\mu
is either on the right side or in the center and by applying Lemma
5.22
, we get

(59)

f_{\mu}(t)=\sum_{d=j}^{i+j}t^{d}=\sum_{d=n-k}^{m+n-s-k}t^{d}.

which gives

(60)

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{m-n}\left(\sum_{\mu\in E(s)}f_{\mu}(t)\right)q^{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{m-n}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\min(s,n)}f_{(s,k)}(t)\right)q^{s}

(61)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{m-n}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\min(s,n)}\sum_{d=n-k}^{m+n-k-s}t^{d}\right)q^{s}.

Case
2
.

We now take
s=m+n-2i
with
0\leq i<n
. By Proposition
5.17
,
\mu\in E(s)
if and only if
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
with
0\leq j\leq i
. In particular,
\mu
is in the center or on the right side and by applying Lemma
5.22
, we get

(62)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(\sum_{\mu\in E(m+n-2i)}f_{\mu}(t)\right)q^{m+n-2i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i}f_{(m-i,n-j)}(t)\right)q^{m+n-2i}

(63)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{d=j}^{i+j}t^{d}\right)q^{m+n-2i}.

Case
3
.

We now take
s=m+n-2j+1
with
1\leq j\leq n
. By Proposition
5.17
,
\mu\in E(s)
if and only if
\mu=(m-i,n-j)
with
0\leq i<j
. In particular,
\mu
is on the left side and by applying Lemma
5.22
, we get

(64)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{\mu\in E(m+n-2j+1)}f_{\mu}(t)\right)q^{m+n-2j+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}f_{(m-i,n-j)}(t)\right)q^{m+n-2j+1}

(65)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\sum_{d=i}^{i+j}t^{d}\right)q^{m+n-2j+1}.

∎

Our goal is now to compute
A_{m,n}-A_{m-1,n-1}
for all triangular partition
\lambda=(m,n)
such that
n>0
. The formula for
A_{\lambda}
is given on Theorem
5.8
. It is a sum of Schur functions which can be obtained by a certain enumeration of semi-standard Young tableaux as expressed in (
1
). We introduce a few notations which are going to be useful.

Definition 5.24
.

We write
\bm{S}_{m,n}
the set of semi-standard Young tableau of shape
m,n
whose values are taken in
\{1,2,3\}
. If
\pi\in\bm{S}_{m,n}
, we write
\pi_{i}
the number of occurrences of the value
i
in the tableau.

We write

(66)

\bm{A}_{m,n}:=\bigcup_{d=0}^{\min(n,m-n)}\bm{S}_{m+n-2d,d}.

In particular, we have

(67)

A_{m,n}(q,t,1)=\sum_{\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}}q^{\pi_{1}}t^{\pi_{2}}.

The first step of our computation is to show that some terms of
A_{m,n}-A_{m-1,n-1}
cancel just like in
P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}
.

Proposition 5.25
.

Let
\lambda=(m,n)
be a triangular partition such that
n>0
. Let
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m-1,n-1}
a tableau of shape
(b,a)
, we write
\varphi(\pi)
the tableau of shape
(b+2,a)
such that the labels of the
2
new cells are
3
. Then
\varphi(\pi)\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. In other words,
\varphi(\bm{A}_{m-1,n-1})\subseteq\bm{A}_{m,n}
. Besides, this is a strict inclusion and
\pi^{\prime}\in\varphi(\bm{A}_{m-1,n-1})
if and only if
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d\leq n-1
and the last two cells of the lower row of
\pi^{\prime}
are labeled by
3
. We write
\NewB_{m,n}=\bm{A}_{m,n}\setminus\varphi(\bm{A}_{m-1,n-1})
and call
new
tableaux the elements of
\NewB_{m,n}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m-1,n-1}
. Then
\pi
is of shape
((m-1)+(n-1)-2d,d)
with
d\leq\min(n-1,m-1-n+1)=\min(n-1,m-n)
. We have that
\varphi(\pi)
is of shape
m+n-2d,d
and as
d\leq n-1<n
and
d\leq m-n
, we obtain
\varphi(\pi)\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
.

Now take
\pi^{\prime}\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. Then
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
(m+n-2d,d)
with
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
. Suppose that
d\leq n-1
. As we have also
d\leq m-n
, we get that
d\leq\min(n-1,(m-1)-(n-1))
and so by Remark
4.11
,
m+n-2-d,d
is a well defined partition. It means that we can remove two cells from the lower row of
\pi^{\prime}
and obtain a tableau
\pi
of
\bm{A}_{m-1,n-1}
. If these two cells were labeled by
3
, we have
\pi^{\prime}=\varphi(\pi)
.

On the other hand, if the last two cells of the lower row of
\pi^{\prime}
are not labeled by
3
, by definition it is not the image of any tableau by
\varphi
. Besides, if
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d>n-1
(the only possility then is
d=n
), by removing two cells on the lower row, we obtain a shape that is not in
\bm{A}_{m-1,n-1}
as the condition on
d
is not satisfied.
∎

By definition if
\pi^{\prime}=\varphi(\pi)
we have that
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=\pi_{1}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{2}
so the term
q^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}t^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}
in
A_{m,n}(q,t,1)
is canceled by the term
q^{\pi_{1}}t^{\pi_{2}}
in
A_{m-1,n-1}(q,t,1)
. So the difference
A_{m,n}-A_{m-1,n-1}
in
q,t,1
can be computed by enumerating the tableaux of
\NewB_{m,n}
.

In the following, we always assume that
(m,n)
is a triangular partition and that
n>0
.

Definition 5.26
.

Let
\pi
be a new tableau of
\bm{A}_{m,n}
. We say that a cell
c
of
\pi
is
raisable
if it is on the lower row, labeled by
2
and with at least
n
cells to its left. We write
r(\pi)
the number of raisable cells of
\pi
and
R
the subset of
\NewB_{m,n}
formed by the tableaux containing at least one raisable cell. If
\pi
is in
R
, we define
\psi(\pi)
in following way.

(1)

If the lower row of
\pi
does not contain any
3
: we replace the last cell of the lower row by a
3
.

(2)

If the lower row contains a
3
and if
\pi
is of shape
m-n,n
, we replace the last raisable cell (on the lower row) by a
3
.

(3)

If the lower row contains a
3
and if
\pi
is not of shape
m-n,n
, let
(b,a)
be the shape of
\pi
, then
\psi(\pi)
is the tableau of shape
(b-2,a+1)
such that the extra cell on the upper row is labeled by
3
and all the other remaining labels are kept unchanged.

We show some examples on Figure
32
applying
\psi
on some elements of
\NewB_{7,2}
. On the first example, the lower row does not contain any
3
: we are in Case (
1
) and we replace the last
2
by a
3
. On the second example, the lower row contains some
3
but we the shape of
\pi
is
(5,2)=(m-n,n)
: we are in Case (
2
)
and replace the last
2
by a
3
. On the third example, the lower row contains one
3
and the shape is not
(5,2)
. We are in Case (
3
), we remove the last two cells of the lower row (
2
and
3
) and add a
3
on the upper row. Finally, on the fourth example, the tableau does not contain any raisable cell as the only
2
on the lower row has only one cell to its left. More examples are shown on Figure
33
.

.
Example of
\psi
on some tableaux of
\NewB_{7,2}

Proposition 5.27
.

Let
\pi
be tableau of
\NewB_{m,n}
. Then
\pi^{\prime}=\psi(\pi)
is also in
\NewB_{m,n}
, and we have
r(\pi^{\prime})=r(\pi)-1
and
\pi^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{2}-1
.

Proof.

First note that
\pi
is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
. As a raisable cell has at least
n
cells to its left and
d\leq n
, it means that a raisable cell never has a cell above it.

Case (
1
).

Suppose first that the lower row of
\pi
does not contain any
3
. As
\pi
contains at least one raisable cell, it contains some cells labeled by
2
on its lower row. In particular the last cell of the lower row is labeled by
2
and
\pi^{\prime}
is obtained by replacing this label by a
3
. The tableau
\pi^{\prime}
is of the same shape than
\pi
and is still a semi-standard Young tableau so
\pi^{\prime}\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. Besides,
\pi^{\prime}
has only one cell labeled by
3
on its lower row (the one we have just created) so
\pi^{\prime}\in\NewB_{m,n}
. As we have replaced a
2
by a
3
, we clearly have
\pi^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{2}-1
. The cell we have changed to
3
was by hypothesis a raisable cell and the other cells have not been changed so the number of raisable cells is reduced by one.

Case (
2
).

Suppose now that
\pi
is of shape
m-n,n
. In this case,
\pi^{\prime}
is also of the same shape than
\pi
and is still a semi-standard Young tableau. Indeed, the cell whose label we have changed to
3
has no cell above it as it is raisable and is followed by cells also labeled by
3
. So
\pi^{\prime}\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. Besides, as
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d=n>n-1
, we have
\pi^{\prime}\in\NewB_{m,n}
. As we have changed a
2
into a
3
, we clearly have
\pi^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{2}-1
and as before the number of raisable cells has been decreased by one.

Case (
3
).

Now suppose that
\pi
is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d\neq n
,
i.e.
,
d\leq n-1
and contains at least a
3
on its lower row. Let us call
y
and
z
the last two cells of the lower row. By hypothesis,
z
is labeled by
3
. Now if
y
is labeled by
3
, then the last two cells are and
\pi\not\in\NewB_{m,n}
as
d\leq n-1
by hypothesis. If
y
is labeled by
1
,
\pi
does not contain any raisable cell. So
y
is labeled by
2
and is raisable. In particular, it has at least
n
cells to its left which gives that
m+n-2d\geq n+2
.

The tableau
\pi^{\prime}
is obtained by removing
y
and
z
and by adding an extra cell
y^{\prime}
on the upper line labeled by
3
. As
y
is raisable by hypothesis, it contains at least
n
cells to its left, all with labels smaller than or equal to
2
. As
d<n
,
y^{\prime}
is added above a cell to the left of
y
and we obtain a well defined semi-standard Young tableau of shape
(m+n-2(d+1),d+1)
. By hypothesis, we have that
d+1\leq n
. Suppose that
d+1>m-n
. As
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
, this implies that
\min(n,m-n)=m-n
and
d=m-n
. As
(m,n)
is triangular, this can happen only if
m
is equal to
2n-1
and so
m-n=n-1
. We find that
m+n-2d=n+1
which contradicts the fact that
y
is a raisable cell.

We then have
d+1\leq m-n
. This gives that
d+1\leq\min(n,m-n)
and so
\pi^{\prime}\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. More over, as no cell on the lower row of
\pi^{\prime}
is labeled by
3
, we have
\pi^{\prime}\in\NewB_{m,n}
. We have removed exactly one cell labeled by
2
which was also raisable so the result is proved.
∎

Proposition 5.28
.

We call
R^{\prime}
the subset of
\NewB_{m,n}
formed by the tableaux containing at least one value
3
. Let
\pi^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
, we define
\psi^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime})
as follows:

•

if
\pi^{\prime}
contains at least one
3
on its lower row, replace the first value
3
with a
2
;

•

otherwise, if
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
(b,a)
, then
\psi^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime})
is the tableau of shape
(b+2,a-1)
where the last cell of the upper row has been removed and two cells labeled by
2
and
3
have been added on the lower row.

Then
\psi^{\prime}=\psi^{-1}
. In other words,
\psi
is a bijection between
R
and
R^{\prime}
.

Proof.

As
\psi
always adds a cell labeled by
3
, it is clear that
\psi(R)\subseteq R^{\prime}
. It is also clearly injective. Indeed if
\psi
changes a
2
into a
3
on the lower row, then
\psi^{\prime}
changes it back into a
2
. If
\psi
removes two cells from the lower row and adds a
3
on the upper row, then we have seen that all the remaining cells of the lower row are labeled by
1
or
2
and that the two removed cells were labeled by
2
and
3
:
\psi^{\prime}
reverse the operation.

We have to show that
\psi
is surjective and that its inverse is
\psi^{\prime}
. Let
\pi^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
. In particular,
\pi^{\prime}\in\NewB_{m,n}
and is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
. We write
\pi:=\psi^{\prime}(\pi)
, in each of the cases below, we show that
\pi
belongs to
R
and that
\psi(\pi)=\pi^{\prime}
following the different cases of Definition
5.26
.

Goal:
\pi
belongs to Case (
1
).

Suppose first that
\pi^{\prime}
contains at least one
3
on its lower row and that
d\leq n-1
. As
\pi^{\prime}
is a new tableau, by Proposition
5.25
, it contains exactly one
3
on its lower row: otherwise the last two cells would be labeled by
3
. So
\pi
is the tableau of the same shape than
\pi^{\prime}
where this single
3
has been replaced by a
2
. In particular
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. The last cell of the lower row is now labeled by
2
and we claim that it is raisable. Indeed as we have
d\leq n-1
, we have
m+n-2d\geq m-n+2
. Besides, as
(m,n)
is triangular, we have by Remark
4.2
that
m-n\geq n-1
and we obtain
m+n-2d\geq n+1
. So the last cell of the lower row has at least
n
cells to its left. We have that
\pi
is in
R
. Besides, it does not contain any
3
on its lower row so we are in Case (
1
) and its image by
\psi
is indeed
\pi^{\prime}
.

Goal:
\pi
belongs to Case (
2
)

Suppose now that
\pi^{\prime}
contains at least one
3
on its lower row but is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d>n-1
. The only possibility is
d=n
and so
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
m-n,n
. We call
y
the first cell of the lower row of
\pi^{\prime}
labeled by a
3
. Note that as
\pi^{\prime}
is a semi-standard Young tableau with labels in
\{1,2,3\}
, this means that
y
has no cell above it. As
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
m-n,n
, this implies that
y
has at least
n
cells to its left. Now
\pi
is the tableau where we replace the label of
y
by a
2
. As
\pi
has the same shape that
\pi^{\prime}
, clearly
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. As it is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d>n-1
, it is also in
\NewB_{m,n}
. Finally, the cell
y
is raisable in
\pi
so
\pi\in R
and we are in Case
2
with
\psi(\pi)=\pi^{\prime}
.

Goal:
\pi
belongs to Case
3
.

We now suppose that
\pi^{\prime}
does not contain any
3
on its lower row. As
\pi^{\prime}\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
by hypothesis, in particular
\pi^{\prime}
is of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
and as it contains at least one cell in its upper row, then
d>0
. Then
\pi
is of shape
m+n-2(d-1),d-1
and
0\leq d-1<d\leq\min(n,m-n)
so
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}
. The two cells added on the lower row in
\pi
are labeled by
2
and
3
so the lower row does not end with 2 values
3
and
\pi\in\NewB_{m,n}
. Let us call
y
the added cell labeled by
2
. By construction it has
m+n-2d
cells to its left. As
d\leq n
and
d\leq m-n
, we write
m+n-2d=m+n-d-d\geq m+n-n-(m-n)=n
. The cell
y
has at least
n
cells to its left and is labeled by
2
: it is raisable and
\pi\in R
. Besides,
\pi
contains a
3
on its lower row by construction and is not of shape
m-n,n
because
d-1<n
: we are in Case
3
and
\psi(\pi)=\pi^{\prime}
.
∎

\cline{1-6}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-6}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

6

\begin{aligned} {\raisebox{-2.58334pt}{$\begin{array}[t]{*{8}c}\cline{1-8}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-8}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

\rightarrow

\begin{aligned} {\raisebox{-2.58334pt}{$\begin{array}[t]{*{8}c}\cline{1-8}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-8}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

\rightarrow

\begin{aligned} {\raisebox{-2.58334pt}{$\begin{array}[t]{*{6}c}\cline{1-1}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-6}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-6}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

\begin{aligned} {\raisebox{-2.58334pt}{$\begin{array}[t]{*{6}c}\cline{1-1}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-6}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-6}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

7

\begin{aligned} {\raisebox{-2.58334pt}{$\begin{array}[t]{*{8}c}\cline{1-8}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$2$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-8}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

\rightarrow

\begin{aligned} {\raisebox{-2.58334pt}{$\begin{array}[t]{*{8}c}\cline{1-8}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$3$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-8}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

8

\begin{aligned} {\raisebox{-2.58334pt}{$\begin{array}[t]{*{8}c}\cline{1-8}\cr\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule&\vrule\hskip 2.58334pt\lx@intercol\hfil\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$1$}\hfil\hskip 2.58334pt\vrule\\
\cline{1-8}\cr\end{array}$}}\end{aligned}

Figure 33
.
New tableaux of
\bm{A}_{6,2}
generated through the action of
\psi
.

Proposition 5.29
.

Let
\pi^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
and let
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
be the subset of
\bm{A}_{m,n}
of tableaux with no label
3
, then there exists a unique tableau
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
such that
\pi^{\prime}
is the image of
\pi
through multiple applications of
\psi
. More precisely, we have
\pi^{\prime}=\psi^{(k)}(\pi)
with
k=r(\pi)-r(\pi^{\prime})
.

This is illustrated on Figure
33
: we see that all new tableaux of
\bm{A}_{6,2}
can be obtained by applying
\psi
a certain amount of time on a tableau containing only
1
and
2
. The tableaux are ordered by the number of
1
they contain.

Proof.

Note first that
\NewB_{m,n}\setminus R^{\prime}=\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
. Indeed, if a tableau does not contain any
3
, it is necessarily a new tableau.

Now remember that by Proposition
5.28
,
\psi
is a bijection between
R
and
R^{\prime}
. So there is a unique tableau
\pi=\psi^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime})
in
R
such that
\pi^{\prime}=\psi(\pi)
. Besides, by Proposition
5.27

r(\pi)=r(\pi^{\prime})+1
. If
\pi\not\in R^{\prime}
, then
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
and the result is proved. Otherwise, we can apply
\psi^{\prime}
again until we reach an element outside of
R^{\prime}
: the process necessarily ends because the number of raisable cells is increased at each step. Beside the number of times
\psi
needs to be applied is given by difference in the number of raisable cells.
∎

Corollary 5.30
.

We have

(68)

A_{m,n}(q,t,1)-A_{m-1,n-1}(q,t,1)=\sum_{\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}}q^{\pi_{1}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}\right)

Proof.

Thanks to Proposition
5.25
, we have that

(69)

A_{m,n}(q,t,1)-A_{m-1,n-1}(q,t,1)=\sum_{\pi\in\NewB_{m,n}}q^{\pi_{1}}t^{\pi_{2}}.

If
\pi\in\NewB_{m,n}\setminus R^{\prime}
, then
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
and the term
q^{\pi_{1}}t^{\pi_{2}}
corresponds to the
k=0
term in (
68
).

Now let
\pi^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}
,
i.e.
,
\pi^{\prime}\in\NewB_{m,n}
and it contains at least one cell labeled by
3
. The monomial corresponding to
\pi^{\prime}
in the sum is
q^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}t^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}
. By Proposition
5.29
, there is a unique
\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
such that
\pi^{\prime}=\psi^{(k)}(\pi)
with
k=r(\pi)-r(\pi^{\prime})
. In particular
1\leq k\leq r(\pi)
. As the number of
1
is not changed by
\psi
, we have
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=\pi_{1}
. The number of
2
is reduced by
1
at each application of
\psi
so
\pi^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{2}-k
. The term
q^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}t^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}
corresponds to the
k=r(\pi)-r(\pi^{\prime})
in the sum of (
68
).
∎

This last results tells us that to perform our computation, we need to enumerate the tableaux of
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
. They are actually very easy to construct (as we have seen in Section
4.3
). We express the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.31
.

Let
m,n
be a triangular partition and
d
such that
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
. Then, for all
s
with
0\leq s\leq m+n
, there exists a tableau
\pi
in
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
\pi_{1}=s
if and only if
d\leq s\leq m+n-2d
. If it exists, this is the only tableau of
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
with this shape and we have
r(\pi)=m+n-2d-\max(s,n)
and
\pi_{2}=m+n-d-s
.

Proof.

The condition of a semi-standard Young tableau makes it clear than once the shape is fixed, there is at most one possible tableau
\pi
with a given number of
1
: writes the
s
values
1
on the lower row and fill the rest of the tableau with the value
2
. For this construction to be possible, the lower row needs to be at least of size
s
, which gives
s\leq m+n-2d
. Besides, for the tableau to be semi-stadard, each cell of the upper row needs to be above a cell labeled by
1
: this gives
d\leq s
.

The raisable cells are the cells of the lower row after the first
n
cells and labeled by
2
. To count them, we thus need to remove either
n
or
s
to the length of the row, which gives
r(\pi)=m+n-2d-\max(n,s)
.

The number of cells labeled by
2
is the total number of cells minus
s
,
i.e.
,
\pi_{2}=m+n-2d+d-s=m+n-d-s

∎

We are almost ready to prove our main equality but we first need to state a small general enumeration trick which we will be using multiple times.

Lemma 5.32
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
with
a\leq b
then

(70)

\sum_{i=0}^{a}\sum_{j=0}^{b}t^{i+j}=\sum_{i=0}^{a}\sum_{j=0}^{b+a-2i}t^{i+j}.

Proof.

Note that as
a\leq b
, the sum between
j=0
and
j=b+a-2i
is well defined with at least one term because
b+a-2i\geq b-a\geq 0
. This allows for classical summation of geometric series and we find that both expressions are equal to

(71)

\frac{1-t^{a+1}-t^{b+1}+t^{a+b+2}}{(1-t)^{2}}.

∎

Remark 5.33
.

Beside the easy computational explanation, there is also a nice combinatorial interpretation of these sums as we illustrate on Figure
34
. They both enumerate entries in a
2
dimensional array. The first sum does a classical enumerates line by line while the second sum enumerates first a ribbon going through the first line and last column, then the ribbon underneath and so on.

0

1

2

\dots

b

1

2

3

\dots

b+1

2

3

4

\dots

b+2

\dots

a

a+1

a+2

\dots

a+b

0

1

2

\dots

b

1

2

3

\dots

b

b+1

2

3

4

\dots

b

b+1

b+2

\dots

\dots

a

a+1

a+2

\dots

b

b+a

Figure 34
.
Illustration of two sum enumerations.

Proposition 5.34
.

Let
(m,n)
be a triangular partition with
n>0
, then

(72)

A_{m,n}(q,t,1)-A_{m-1,n-1}(q,t,1)=P_{m,n}(q,t)-P_{m-1,n-1}(q,t).

Proof.

Following the formula of Proposition
5.23
, we are going to split the sum (
68
) depending on the power of
q
and show that we get an equality in each case. The power of
q
corresponds to the number of
1
in the tableaux. It varies between
0
and
m+n
. We can split the proof into three cases following the
3
possibilities of Proposition
5.17
.

Case
0\leq s\leq m-n
.

Let us fix the power of
q
to a certain value
s
with
0\leq s\leq m-n
. The coefficient of
q^{s}
is a polynomial in
t
. In
P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}
it is given in (
51
). In
A_{m,n}(q,t,1)-A_{m-1,n-1}(q,t,1)
, it is given by an enumeration on tableaux
\pi
such that
\pi_{1}=s
. We thus need to prove

(73)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}\\
\pi_{1}=s\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\min(s,n)}\sum_{v=n-k}^{m+n-k-s}t^{v}.

(74)

\displaystyle=t^{n-\min(s,n)}\sum_{i=0}^{\min(s,n)}\sum_{j=0}^{m-s}t^{i+j}.

The second line is obtained by a simple change of variable
i=\min(s,n)-k
to get something in the form of Lemma
5.32
. Note that by hypothesis, we have
s\leq m-n
which implies
n\leq m-s
and so
\min(s,n)\leq m-s
, so Lemma
5.32
can be applied with
a=\min(s,n)
and
b=m-s
.

Now, let us identify the tableaux of
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
such that
\pi_{1}=s
. Using Lemma
5.31
, there is one such tableau for each
d
with
0\leq d\leq\min(n,m-n)
and
d\leq s\leq m+n-2d
. If
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
, in particular
d\leq n
and we have
m+n-2d\geq m-n\geq s
by hypothesis on
s
so this one condition is always satisfied.

We get that for each
d
, with
0\leq d\leq\min(s,n,m-n)
, we have exactly one tableau
\pi
in
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
of shape
m+n-2d,d
with
\pi_{1}=s
. As by hypothesis
s\leq m-n
, we actually get
0\leq d\leq\min(s,n)
. Using the values of
r(\pi)
and
\pi_{2}
given in Lemma
5.31
, we obtain

(75)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}\\
\pi_{1}=s\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d=0}^{\min(s,n)}\sum_{k=0}^{m+n-2d-\max(s,n)}t^{m+n-d-s-k}

(76)

\displaystyle=\sum_{d=0}^{\min(s,n)}\sum_{v=\max(s,n)-s}^{m+n-s-2d}t^{d+v}.

by taking
v=m+n-2d-s-k
. Now see that
n-\min(s,n)=\max(s,n)-s
which allows us to rewrite the sum as

(77)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}\\
\pi_{1}=s\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}

\displaystyle=t^{n-\min(s,n)}\sum_{i=0}^{\min(s,n)}\sum_{j=0}^{m-s+\min(s,n)-2d}t^{i+j}.

We conclude using Lemma
5.32
with
a=\min(s,n)
and
b=m-s
. Note that this enumeration is illustrated on an example on Figure
33
for
m=6
and
n=2
(lines
s=0
to
s=4
).

Case
s>m-n
and of same parity as
m+n
.

As we have seen in Proposition
5.17
, then
s=m+n-2i
with
0\leq i\leq n-1
. The coefficient of
q^{s}
in
P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}
is a polynomial in
t
given in (
52
). We need to prove

(78)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}\\
\pi_{1}=s\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{d=j}^{i+j}t^{d}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}\sum_{v=0}^{i}t^{j+v}.

Let us identify the tableaux of
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
such that
\pi_{1}=s
. Again, we use Lemma
5.31
. The condition
s\leq m+n-2d
gives
d\leq i
. The other conditions
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
and
d\leq s
are then always satisfied. Indeed, as
i<n
, we have in particular
i\leq m-n
(because
m,n
is triangular so by Remark
4.2
,
n\leq m-n+1
) so
i\leq\min(n,m-n)
. If
d\leq i
, this implies
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
. Besides, by Remark
4.2
,
i,m+n-2i
is a proper partition meaning
i\leq m+n-2i
. Then
d\leq i
also implies
d\leq m+n-2i
.

Now, as
i<n
and
i\leq m-n
, we have
m+n-2i\geq m+n-n-(m-n)=n
so
\max(s,n)=s
. By Lemma
5.31
, we have

(79)

\displaystyle r(\pi)

\displaystyle=m+n-2d-s=2i-2d,

(80)

\displaystyle\pi_{2}

\displaystyle=m+n-d-s=2i-d,

and obtain

(81)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}\\
\pi_{1}=s\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}=\sum_{d=0}^{i}\sum_{k=0}^{2i-2d}t^{2i-d-k}=\sum_{d=0}^{i}\sum_{v=0}^{2i-2d}t^{d+v}.

We conclude again using Lemma
5.32
. This enumeration is illustrated on an example on Figure
33
for
m=6
and
n=2
(lines
s=6
and
s=8
).

Case
s>m-n
and of different parity than
m+n
.

As we have seen in Proposition
5.17
, then
s=m+n-2j+1
with
1\leq j\leq n
. The coefficient of
q^{s}
in
P_{m,n}-P_{m-1,n-1}
is a polynomial in
t
given in (
53
). We need to prove

(82)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}\\
\pi_{1}=s\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\sum_{d=i}^{i+j}t^{d}=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\sum_{v=0}^{j}t^{i+v}.

Let us identify the tableaux of
\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}
such that
\pi_{1}=s
. Again, we use Lemma
5.31
. The condition
s\leq m+n-2d
gives
2d+1\leq 2j
which is equivalent to
d<j
. Again, the other conditions
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
and
d\leq s
are then always satisfied. Remember that by Remark
4.2
,
n\leq m-n+1
. As we have
d<n
, we also have
d\leq m-n
and so
d\leq\min(n,m-n)
. Similarly, we have
j\leq n\leq m-n+1
, so
m+n-2j+1\geq m+n-n-(m-n+1)=n-1\geq d
.

Besides, we also have
m+n-2j+1\geq m+n-2n+1=m-n+1\geq n
so
\max(s,n)=s
and we have

(83)

\displaystyle r(\pi)

\displaystyle=m+n-2d-s=2j-2d-1,

(84)

\displaystyle\pi_{2}

\displaystyle=m+n-d-s=2j-d-1,

and obtain

(85)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\bm{A}_{m,n}^{|1,2|}\\
\pi_{1}=s\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{r(\pi)}t^{\pi_{2}-k}=\sum_{d=0}^{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{2j-2d-1}t^{2j-d-1-k}=\sum_{d=0}^{j-1}\sum_{v=0}^{2j-2d-1}t^{d+v}.

We conclude again using Lemma
5.32
. This enumeration is illustrated on an example on Figure
33
for
m=6
and
n=2
(lines
s=5
and
s=7
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.8
.

This is direct by induction on
n
using Proposition
5.19
for the initial case and Proposition
5.34
for the induction.
∎

Remark 5.35
.

Note that even though we prove that both expressions are equal, we have to use some enumeration trick and do not exhibit a direct bijection between intervals and semi-standard tableaux. Indeed, we have found that the “natural”way to enumerate intervals is to fix the maximal element. But this enumeration does not translate well into semi-standard tableaux which is why we have to use Lemma
5.32
to prove the equality. In the end, it is not clear which tableau would “naturally” correspond to which interval. This explains why we have no interpretation of the parameter
r
(the number of values equal to
3
in the tableaux) on the interval size.

6.
Conclusion

To our knowledge, Theorem
5.8
is the first to formally exhibit a link between enumeration of intervals in Tamari-like lattices and Schur functions as all results in the general case (even for the classical Tamari lattice) are only conjectural. For this reason, we believe it is an important result and more so as it opens to Conjecture
3
. Nevertheless, we see that even in the “simple” case of
2
-partitions, the proof of the equality is rather technical and does not rely on a bijection as we have explained in our previous remark.
